# Iranian Revolution

"1979 Revolution" redirects here. For the video game based on the events, see [*1979 Revolution: Black Friday*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1979_Revolution:_Black_Friday). For other uses, see [Iranian Revolution (disambiguation)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_Revolution_\(disambiguation\)).

| Iranian Revolution |  |
| --- | --- |
| Mass demonstrations of people protesting against [the Shah](20065598.md) and the [Pahlavi government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pahlavi_Iran) on the day of [Ashura](488563.md) on 11 December 1978 at College Bridge, [Tehran](57654.md) |  |
| Date | 7 January 1978 (1978-01-07) – 11 February 1979 (1979-02-11) (1 year, 1 month and 4 days) |
| Location | [Imperial State of Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pahlavi_Iran) |
| Caused by | Discontent with [Mohammad Reza Pahlavi](20065598.md)'s rule [Repression and torture of dissidents](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_rights_in_Pahlavi_Iran) [Exile of Ruhollah Khomeini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ruhollah_Khomeini's_life_in_exile) ([Shia Islamist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shia_Islamism) faction) *[and others](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Background_and_causes_of_the_Iranian_Revolution)* |
| Goals | Overthrow of the [Pahlavi dynasty](338883.md) |
| Methods | [Demonstrations](6787314.md) [Strikes](41138909.md) [Civil resistance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_resistance) [Riots](146704.md)[^cite_note-1] [Rebellion](513246.md) [Armed street fighting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Street_fighting)[^cite_note-2] |
| Result | Establishment of the Islamic Republic of Iran |

| [v](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Template\:Mohammad_Reza_Pahlavi_series)[t](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Template_talk\:Mohammad_Reza_Pahlavi_series)[e](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:EditPage/Template\:Mohammad_Reza_Pahlavi_series) |

The **Iranian Revolution**,[^cite_note-5] also known as the **Islamic Revolution**,[^cite_note-6] culminated in the overthrow of the [Pahlavi dynasty](338883.md) in 1979. The revolution led to the replacement of the [Imperial State of Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pahlavi_Iran) by the [Islamic Republic of Iran](14653.md), as the monarchical government of Shah [Mohammad Reza Pahlavi](20065598.md) was superseded by [Ruhollah Khomeini](26232.md), an [Islamist](15012.md) cleric who had headed one of the rebel factions, and became the country’s supreme leader.[^cite_note-\:0-7]<sup>: 154 </sup>The ousting of Mohammad Reza Pahlavi, the last [Shah of Iran](13877.md), formally marked the end of [Iran's historical monarchy](13877.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGölz2017229-8]

In 1953, the [CIA](5183633.md) and [MI6](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MI6)-backed [1953 Iranian coup d'état](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1953_Iranian_coup_d'%C3%A9tat) overthrew Iran's prime minister, [Mohammad Mossadegh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammad_Mosaddegh), who had nationalized the [Anglo-Persian Oil Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglo-Persian_Oil_Company).[^cite_note-9][^cite_note-10][^cite_note-11] The coup reinstated Mohammad Reza Pahlavi as an absolute monarch and significantly increased the level of influence of the [United States](3434750.md) over Iran.[^cite_note-ABC-News-2020-12]

In 1963, the Shah had launched the [White Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Revolution), a top-down modernization and land reform program that alienated many sectors of society, especially the clergy. Khomeini emerged as a vocal critic and was exiled in 1964. However, as ideological tensions persisted between Pahlavi and Khomeini, anti-government demonstrations began in October 1977, developing into a campaign of civil resistance that included [communists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communists), [socialists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialism_in_Iran), and [Islamists](15012.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian1982479-13][^cite_note-Afkhami-14][^cite_note-15] Mass protests were underway. A key turning point occurred on 19 August 1978, when the [Cinema Rex fire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinema_Rex_fire) by Islamic militants killed around 400 people.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmanat2017719-16][^cite_note-17] However a large portion of the public believed it was a [false flag](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/False_flag) operation by [SAVAK](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SAVAK), the Shah's [secret police](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secret_police), to discredit the opposition and justify a crackdown, fueling nationwide outrage and mobilization. By the end of 1978, the revolution had become a broad-based uprising that paralyzed the country for the remainder of that year.[^cite_note-mottahedeh-18][^cite_note-19]

On 16 January 1979, Pahlavi left Iran for exile,[^cite_note-20] leaving his duties to Iran's [Regency Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regency_Council_\(Iran\)) and [Shapour Bakhtiar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shapour_Bakhtiar), the opposition-based prime minister. On 1 February 1979, [Khomeini returned](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ruhollah_Khomeini's_return_to_Iran), following an invitation by the government;[^cite_note-Milani_Shah-21][^cite_note-Milani-Persians-22] several million greeted him as he landed in [Tehran](57654.md).[^cite_note-23] By 11 February, the monarchy was brought down and Khomeini assumed leadership while guerrillas and rebel troops overwhelmed Pahlavi loyalists in armed combat.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGraham1980228-24][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKurzman2004111-25] Following the [March 1979 Islamic Republic referendum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1979_Iranian_Islamic_Republic_referendum), in which 98% approved the shift to an [Islamic republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_republic), the new government began drafting the present-day [constitution of the Islamic Republic of Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Iran);[^cite_note-26][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian1982479-13][^cite_note-Afkhami-14][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKurzman2004-27][^cite_note-Amuzegar-28] Khomeini emerged as the absolute [supreme leader of Iran](36814344.md) in December 1979.[^cite_note-29]

The revolution was fueled by widespread perceptions of the Shah's regime as corrupt, repressive, and overly reliant on foreign powers, particularly the United States and the United Kingdom, at the expense of Iran's sovereignty and cultural identity.[^cite_note-30][^cite_note-31] However others perceived the revolution's success as unusual,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmuzegar19914,_9–12-32] since it lacked customary causes of revolutionary sentiment, e.g. defeat in war, financial crisis, [peasant rebellion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_peasant_revolts), or disgruntled military.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEArjomand1988191-33] It occurred in a country experiencing relative prosperity,[^cite_note-Milani_Shah-21][^cite_note-Amuzegar-28] produced profound change at great speed,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmuzegar199110-34] and resulted in a massive exile that characterizes a large portion of [Iranian diaspora](9573713.md),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKurzman2004121-35] and replaced a pro-Western secular[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmanat2017897-36] but authoritarian monarchy[^cite_note-Milani_Shah-21] with an [anti-Western](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Western_sentiment) Islamic republic[^cite_note-Milani_Shah-21][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKurzman2004-27][^cite_note-Amuzegar-28][^cite_note-Middle_East_Studies_1987,_p._261-37] based on the concept of *Velâyat-e Faqih* ([Guardianship of the Islamic Jurist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guardianship_of_the_Islamic_Jurist)), straddling between [authoritarianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Authoritarianism) and [totalitarianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Totalitarianism).[^cite_note-38] In addition to declaring the destruction of [Israel](9282173.md) as a core objective,[^cite_note-39][^cite_note-40] post-revolutionary Iran aimed to undermine the influence of [Sunni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunni_Islam) leaders in the region by supporting [Shi'ite](26961.md) political ascendancy and exporting [Khomeinist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khomeinism) doctrines abroad.[^cite_note-41] In the [aftermath of the revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aftermath_of_the_Iranian_Revolution), Iran began to back Shia militancy across the region to expand its influence in the [Arab world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_world), seeking to establish an Iranian-led Shia political and military order in the Middle East.[^cite_note-42]

## Names

While the name "Iranian Revolution"[^cite_note-43] is common, it is also referred to as the **1979 Revolution**, the **Islamic Revolution**[^cite_note-45] or the **Islamic Revolution of 1979**.

## Background (1891–1977)

Main article: [Background and causes of the Iranian Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Background_and_causes_of_the_Iranian_Revolution)

Reasons advanced for the revolution and its [populist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Populist), [nationalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nationalist), and later [Shia Islamic](26961.md) character include:

1. A backlash against forced Westernization and [imperialism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imperialism);

2. The [1953 Iranian *coup d'état*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1953_Iranian_coup_d'%C3%A9tat);

3. A rise in expectations created by the [1973 oil revenue windfall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1973_oil_crisis);

4. An overly ambitious economic program;

5. Autocratic Rule;

6. Anger over a short, sharp [economic contraction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_contraction) in 1977–1978; and

   [\[Note 4\]](#cite_note-50)

7. Other shortcomings of the previous regime.

The Shah's regime was seen as oppressive, brutal,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarney199837,_47,_67,_128,_155,_167-51][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian1982437-52] corrupt, and lavish by some of the society's classes at that time.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarney199837,_47,_67,_128,_155,_167-51][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMackey1996236,_260-53] It also suffered from functional failures that brought economic [bottlenecks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bottleneck_\(production\)), [shortages](2364729.md), and inflation.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGraham198019,_96-54] The Shah was perceived by many as beholden to—if not a puppet of—a non-Muslim [Western power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_power) (i.e., the United States)[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrumberg2001\[\[Category\:Wikipedia_articles_needing_page_number_citations_from_January_2022\]\]<sup_class="noprint_Inline-Template_"_style="white-space\:nowrap;">\[<i>\[\[Wikipedia\:Citing_sources|<span_title="This_citation_requires_a_reference_to_the_specific_page_or_range_of_pages_in_which_the_material_appears. (January_2022)">page needed</span>\]\]</i>\]</sup>-55][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShirley1997207-56] whose culture was affecting that of Iran. At the same time, support for the Shah may have waned among Western politicians and media—especially under the [administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency_of_Jimmy_Carter) of US President [Jimmy Carter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jimmy_Carter)—as a result of the Shah's support for [OPEC petroleum price increases](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1973_oil_crisis) earlier in the decade.[^cite_note-57] When President Carter enacted a [human rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human-rights) policy which said that countries guilty of human rights violations would be deprived of American arms or aid, this helped give some Iranians the courage to post open letters and [petitions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petition) in the hope that the repression by the government might subside.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKeddie2003214-58]

The revolution that substituted the monarchy of [Mohammad Reza Pahlavi](20065598.md) with [Islam](6037917.md) and [Khomeini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khomeini) is credited in part to the spread of the [Shi'a](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shi'a) version of the [Islamic revival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_revival). While often described mainly as a religious revolt, the revolution also grew from a mix of nationalist goals, political populism, and religious radicalism.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian2008-59] It resisted [westernization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Westernization) and saw [Ayatollah](63899.md) Khomeini as following in the footsteps of the Shi'a [Imam](15240.md) [Husayn ibn Ali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Husayn_ibn_Ali), with the Shah playing the role of Husayn's foe, the hated tyrant [Yazid I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yazid_I).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETaheri1985238-60] Other factors include the underestimation of Khomeini's [Islamist](15012.md) movement by both the Shah's advisors—who considered them a minor threat compared to the [Marxists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Left-wing_guerrilla_groups_of_Iran) and [Islamic socialists](285632.md)[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMoin2000178-61][^cite_note-Hoveyda_2003_22-62][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian1982533–534-63]—and by the [secularist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secularist) opponents of the government—who thought the [Khomeinists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khomeinism) could be sidelined.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESchirazi1997293–34-64]

### Tobacco Protest (1891)

Main article: [Tobacco Protest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tobacco_Protest)

At the end of the 19th century, the Shi'a clergy (*[ulama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ulama)*) had a significant influence on [Iranian society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_society). The clergy first showed itself to be a powerful political force in opposition to the monarchy with the 1891 [Tobacco protest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tobacco_protest). On 20 March 1890, the long-standing Iranian monarch [Nasir al-Din Shah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nasir_al-Din_Shah) granted a concession to [British](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Empire) Major G. F. Talbot for a full monopoly over the production, sale, and export of tobacco for 50 years.[^cite_note-k38-65] At the time, the Persian tobacco industry employed over 200,000 people, so the concession represented a major blow to Persian farmers and [bazaaris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bazaari) whose livelihoods were largely dependent on the lucrative tobacco business.[^cite_note-66] The boycotts and protests against it were widespread and extensive as result of [Mirza Hasan Shirazi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mirza_Hasan_Shirazi)'s *[fatwa](11259.md)* (judicial decree).[^cite_note-67] Within two years, Nasir al-Din Shah found himself powerless to stop the popular movement and cancelled the concession.[^cite_note-68]

The Tobacco Protest was the first significant Iranian resistance against Shah and foreign interests, revealing the power of the people and the *ulama* influence among them.[^cite_note-k38-65]

### Persian Constitutional Revolution (1905–1911)

Main article: [Persian Constitutional Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persian_Constitutional_Revolution)

The growing dissatisfaction continued until the Constitutional Revolution of 1905–1911. The revolution led to the establishment of a parliament, the [National Consultative Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Consultative_Assembly) (also known as the *[Majlis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Majlis)*), and approval of the first constitution. Although the constitutional revolution was successful in weakening the [autocracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autocracy) of the [Qajar regime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qajar_dynasty), it failed to provide a powerful alternative government. Therefore, in the decades following the establishment of the new parliament, a number of critical events took place. Many of these events can be viewed as a continuation of the struggle between the constitutionalists and the Shahs of Persia, many of whom were backed by foreign powers against the parliament.

### Reza Shah (1921–1941)

Main article: [Rezā Shāh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rez%C4%81_Sh%C4%81h)

Insecurity and chaos that were created after the Constitutional Revolution led to the rise of General [Reza Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reza_Khan), the commander of the elite [Persian Cossack Brigade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persian_Cossack_Brigade) who seized power in a *coup d'état* in February 1921. He established a [constitutional monarchy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutional_monarchy), deposing the last Qajar [Shah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shah), [Ahmad Shah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahmad_Shah_Qajar), in 1925 and being designated monarch by the National Assembly, to be known thenceforth as Reza Shah, founder of the [Pahlavi dynasty](338883.md).

There were widespread social, economic, and political reforms introduced during his reign, a number of which led to public discontent that would provide the circumstances for the Iranian Revolution. Particularly controversial was the replacement of [Islamic laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fiqh) with secular Western ones and the forbidding of traditional [Islamic clothing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_clothing), [separation of the sexes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sex_segregation_and_Islam), and veiling of women's faces with the [niqab](1322219.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMackey1996184-69] Police forcibly removed and tore the [chadors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chador) off women who resisted his ban on the public [hijab](68301.md).

In 1935, dozens were killed and hundreds injured in the [Goharshad Mosque rebellion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Goharshad_Mosque_rebellion).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBakhash198422-70][^cite_note-FOOTNOTETaheri198594–95-71][^cite_note-72] During the early rise of Reza Shah, [Abdul-Karim Ha'eri Yazdi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdul-Karim_Ha'eri_Yazdi) founded the [Qom Seminary](32825717.md) and created important changes in [seminaries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seminaries). However, he would avoid entering into political issues, as did other religious leaders who followed him. Hence, no widespread anti-government attempts were organized by the clergy during the rule of Reza Shah. The future Ayatollah [Khomeini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khomeini) was a student of Sheikh Abdul Karim Ha'eri.[^cite_note-73]

### Anglo-Soviet invasion and Mohammad Reza Shah (1941–1951)

Main articles: [Anglo-Soviet invasion of Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglo-Soviet_invasion_of_Iran) and [Mohammad Reza Pahlavi](20065598.md)

In 1941, an [invasion by allied British and Soviet troops](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglo-Soviet_invasion_of_Iran) [deposed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deposed) Reza Shah, who was considered friendly to [Nazi Germany](21212.md). They installed his son, [Mohammad Reza Pahlavi](20065598.md) as Shah.[^cite_note-74] Iran remained under Soviet occupation until the [Red Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Army) withdrew in June 1946.[^cite_note-75]

The post-war years were characterized by political instability, as the Shah clashed with the pro-Soviet Prime Minister [Ahmad Qavam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahmad_Qavam), the communist [Tudeh Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tudeh_Party_of_Iran) grew in size and influence and the Iranian Army had to deal with Soviet-sponsored separatist movements in [Iranian Azerbaijan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_Azerbaijan) and [Iranian Kurdistan](61733686.md).[^cite_note-76]

### Mosaddegh and The Anglo-Iranian Oil Company (1951–1952)

Main articles: [Mohammad Mosaddegh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammad_Mosaddegh) and [Anglo-Persian Oil Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglo-Persian_Oil_Company)

From 1901 on, the British Owned [Anglo-Persian Oil Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglo-Persian_Oil_Company) (renamed the Anglo-Iranian Oil Company in 1935), enjoyed a monopoly on the sale and production of Iranian oil. It was the most profitable British business in the world.[^cite_note-77] Most Iranians lived in poverty while the wealth generated from Iranian oil played a decisive role in maintaining Britain as a preeminent global power. In 1951, Iranian Prime Minister [Mohammad Mosaddegh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammad_Mosaddegh) pledged to throw the company out of Iran, reclaim the [petroleum reserves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petroleum_reserves) and free Iran from foreign powers.

In 1952, Mosaddegh [nationalized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nationalized) the Anglo-Iranian Oil Company and became a national hero. The British, however, were outraged and accused him of theft. The British unsuccessfully [sought adjudication](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglo-Iranian_Oil_Co._case) from the [International Court of Justice](14918.md) and the United Nations, sent warships to the [Persian Gulf](24761.md), and finally imposed a crushing [embargo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Embargo). Mosaddegh was unmoved by Britain's campaign against him. One European newspaper, the *[Frankfurter Neue Presse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frankfurter_Neue_Presse)*, reported that Mosaddegh "would rather be fried in Persian oil than make the slightest concession to the British." The British considered an armed invasion, but U.K. Prime Minister [Winston Churchill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Winston_Churchill) decided on a coup after being refused American military support by US President [Harry S. Truman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harry_S._Truman), who sympathized with nationalist movements like Mosaddegh's and had nothing but contempt for old-style [imperialists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imperialists) like those who ran the Anglo-Iranian Oil Company. Mosaddegh, however, learned of Churchill's plans and ordered the [British embassy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Embassy_of_the_United_Kingdom,_Tehran) to be closed in October 1952, forcing all British diplomats and agents to leave the country.

Although the British were initially turned down in their request for American support by President Truman, the election of [Dwight D. Eisenhower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dwight_D._Eisenhower) as US president in November 1952 changed the American stance toward the conflict. This, paired with [Cold War](325329.md) paranoia and fears of communist influence, contributed to American strategic actions. On 20 January 1953, US Secretary of State [John Foster Dulles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Foster_Dulles) and his brother, [C.I.A.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/C.I.A.) Director [Allen Dulles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allen_Dulles), told their British counterparts that they were ready to move against Mosaddegh. In their eyes, any country not decisively allied with the United States was a potential enemy. Iran had immense oil wealth, a long border with the [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union), and a [nationalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_nationalism) prime minister. The prospect of a fall into [communism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communism) and a "second China" (after [Mao Zedong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mao_Zedong) won the [Chinese Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_Civil_War)) terrified the Dulles brothers. [Operation Ajax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Ajax) was born, in which the only [democratic government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_government) Iran ever had was deposed.[^cite_note-Kinzer-2008-78]

### Iranian coup d'état and US influence

Main article: [1953 Iranian coup d'état](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1953_Iranian_coup_d'%C3%A9tat)

On 15 August 1953 a coup d'état was initiated to remove Mosaddegh, with the support of the United States, the United Kingdom and most of the Shia clergy.[^cite_note-Kinzer-2008-78] The Shah fled to Italy when the initial coup attempt on August 15 failed, but returned after a successful second attempt on August 19.[^cite_note-theguardian-79] Mosaddegh was removed from power and put under house arrest, while lieutenant general [Fazlollah Zahedi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fazlollah_Zahedi) was appointed as new Prime Minister by the Shah. The sovereign, who was mainly seen as a figurehead at the time, eventually managed to break free from the shackles of the Iranian elites and impose himself as an autocratic reformist ruler.[^cite_note-80]

The coup reinstated Mohammad Reza Pahlavi as an absolute monarch and significantly increased [United States](3434750.md) influence over Iran. Economically, American firms gained considerable control over Iranian oil production, with US companies taking around 40 percent of the profits. Politically, Iran acted as a counterweight to the [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union) and aligned closely with the [Western Bloc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Bloc). Additionally, the US provided the Shah both the funds and the training for [SAVAK](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SAVAK), Iran's infamous [secret police](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secret_police), with [CIA assistance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CIA_activities_in_Iran).[^cite_note-ABC-News-2020-12][^cite_note-81][^cite_note-82]

[SAVAK](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SAVAK)'s repression unfolded with little scrutiny or challenge from the US. By the late 1970s, popular resistance to the Shah's rule had reached a breaking point.[^cite_note-ABC-News-2020-12][^cite_note-Milani_Shah-21][^cite_note-83]

### White Revolution (1963–1979)

Main article: [White Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Revolution)



Shah Mohammad Reza Pahlavi

The White Revolution was a far-reaching series of reforms in [Iran](14653.md) launched in 1963 by [Shah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shah) Mohammad Reza Pahlavi and lasting until 1979. Mohammad Reza Shah's reform program was built especially to weaken those classes that supported the traditional system. It consisted of several elements including [land reform](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Land_reform); sales of some [state-owned](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State-owned) factories to finance the land reform; the [enfranchisement of women](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Women's_suffrage); [nationalization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nationalization) of forests and pastures; formation of a literacy [corps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corps); and the institution of [profit-sharing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Profit-sharing) schemes for workers in industry.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEArjomand198872–73-84]

The Shah pushed the White Revolution as a step toward [westernization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Westernization),[^cite_note-85] and it was a way for him to [legitimize](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legitimize) the [Pahlavi dynasty](338883.md). Part of the reason for launching the White Revolution was that the Shah hoped to eliminate the influence of landlords and to create a new base of support among the peasants and the working class.[^cite_note-86][^cite_note-87] Thus, the White Revolution in Iran was an attempt to introduce reform from above and preserve traditional power patterns. Through land reform, the essence of the White Revolution, the Shah hoped to ally himself with the [peasantry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peasant) in the countryside, and hoped to sever their ties with the [aristocracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aristocracy) in the city.

The Shah did not foresee the White Revolution leading to new [social tensions](329906.md) that created many of the problems he was trying to avoid. The Shah's reforms more than quadrupled the combined size of the two classes that posed the greatest challenges to his monarchy in the past — the [intelligentsia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intelligentsia), and the urban [working class](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_working_class). Their resentment of the Shah also grew, as they were now stripped of organizations that had represented them in the past, such as political parties, professional associations, trade unions, and independent newspapers. Land reform, instead of allying the peasants with the government, produced large numbers of independent farmers and landless laborers who became part of the political opposition, with no loyalty to the Shah. Many of the masses resented the increasingly corrupt government; their loyalty to the clergy, who were viewed as more concerned with the fate of the populace, remained consistent or increased. As [Ervand Abrahamian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ervand_Abrahamian) pointed out: "The White Revolution had been designed to preempt a [Red Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Revolution). Instead, it paved the way for an Islamic Revolution."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian2008143-88] In theory, oil money funneled to the elite was supposed to create jobs and factories, eventually distributing the money. Instead the wealth tended to remain concentrated in the hands of the very few at the top.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian2008140-89]

### Rise and exile of Ayatollah Khomeini (1963–1979)

Main article: [Ruhollah Khomeini](26232.md)

See also: [Movement of 15 Khordad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Movement_of_15_Khordad) and [Ruhollah Khomeini's return to Iran § Exile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ruhollah_Khomeini's_return_to_Iran#Exile)

The post-revolutionary leader, [Twelver Shia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twelver_Shia) cleric [Ayatollah](63899.md) [Ruhollah Khomeini](26232.md), first rose to political prominence in 1963 when he led opposition to the Shah and his [White Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Revolution). Khomeini was arrested in 1963 after declaring the Shah a "wretched, miserable man" who "embarked on the \[path toward] destruction of Islam in Iran."[^cite_note-90] Three days of major riots throughout Iran followed, with 15,000 dead from police fire as reported by opposition sources.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKhomeiniAlgar198117-91] However, [anti-revolutionary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-revolutionary) sources conjectured that just 32 were killed.[^cite_note-emadbaghi-92]

Khomeini was released after eight months of house arrest and continued his agitation, condemning Iran's close cooperation with Israel and its [capitulations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capitulation_\(treaty\)), or extension of [diplomatic immunity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diplomatic_immunity), to American government personnel in Iran. In November 1964, Khomeini was re-arrested and [sent into exile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ruhollah_Khomeini's_return_to_Iran#Exile) where he remained for 15 years (mostly in [Najaf, Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Najaf,_Iraq)), until the revolution.

#### Ideology of the Iranian Revolution

Main article: [Ideology of the Iranian Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ideology_of_the_Iranian_Revolution)

See also: [Islamic fundamentalism in Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_fundamentalism_in_Iran)



Residents of Tehran participating in the [demonstrations of 5 June 1963](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Movement_of_15_Khordad) with pictures of Ruhollah Khomeini in their hands

In this interim period of "disaffected calm,"[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGraham198069-93] the budding Iranian revival began to undermine the idea of [Westernization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Westernization) as progress that was the basis of the Shah's secular reign, and to form the ideology of the 1979 revolution: [Jalal Al-e-Ahmad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jalal_Al-e-Ahmad)'s idea of *[Gharbzadegi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gharbzadegi)*—that Western culture was a plague or an intoxication to be eliminated;[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMackey1996215,_264–265-94] [Ali Shariati](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ali_Shariati)'s vision of Islam as the one true liberator of the [Third World](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third_World) from oppressive [colonialism](7299.md), [neo-colonialism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neo-colonialism), and [capitalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capitalism);[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKeddie2003201–207-95] and [Morteza Motahhari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Morteza_Motahhari)'s popularized retellings of the Shia faith all spread and gained listeners, readers and supporters.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMackey1996215,_264–265-94]

Most importantly, Khomeini preached that revolt, and especially [martyrdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martyrdom) against injustice and tyranny was part of Shia Islam,[^cite_note-96] and that Muslims should reject the influence of both liberal capitalism and communism, ideas that inspired the revolutionary slogan "Neither East, nor West – Islamic Republic!"

Away from public view, Khomeini developed the ideology of *velayat-e faqih* ([guardianship of the jurist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guardianship_of_the_Islamic_Jurist)) as government, that Muslims—in fact everyone—required "guardianship," in the form of rule or supervision by the leading Islamic jurist or jurists.[^cite_note-97] Such rule was ultimately "more necessary even than prayer and fasting" in Islam,[^cite_note-98] as it would protect Islam from deviation from traditional *[sharia](28840.md)* law and in so doing eliminate poverty, injustice, and the "[plundering](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plundering)" of Muslim land by foreign non-believers.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKhomeiniAlgar198152,_54,_80-99]

This idea of rule by Islamic jurists was spread through his book *[Islamic Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_Government)*, mosque sermons, and smuggled cassette speeches by Khomeini[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETaheri1985196-100][^cite_note-101] among his opposition network of students (*talabeh*), ex-students (able clerics such as [Morteza Motahhari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Morteza_Motahhari), [Mohammad Beheshti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammad_Beheshti), [Mohammad-Javad Bahonar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammad-Javad_Bahonar), [Akbar Hashemi Rafsanjani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akbar_Hashemi_Rafsanjani), and [Mohammad Mofatteh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammad_Mofatteh)), and traditional businessmen (*[bazaari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bazaari)*) inside Iran.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETaheri1985196-100]

#### Opposition groups and organizations

Main article: [Organizations of the Iranian Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organizations_of_the_Iranian_Revolution)

Other opposition groups included [constitutionalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutionalist) liberals—the democratic, reformist Islamic [Freedom Movement of Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_Movement_of_Iran), headed by [Mehdi Bazargan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mehdi_Bazargan), and the more secular [National Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Front_of_Iran). They were based in the urban middle class, and wanted the Shah to adhere to the [Iranian Constitution of 1906](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_Constitution_of_1906) rather than to replace him with a theocracy,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian1982502–503-102] but lacked the cohesion and organization of Khomeini's forces.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKurzman2004144–145-103]

[Communist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communist) groups—primarily the [Tudeh Party of Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tudeh_Party_of_Iran) and the [Fedaian guerrillas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organization_of_Iranian_People's_Fedaian_\(Majority\))[^cite_note-105]—had been weakened considerably by government repression. Despite this the guerrillas did help play an important part in the final February 1979 overthrow[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKurzman2004145–146-106] delivering "the regime its [coup de grace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coup_de_grace)."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian1982495-107] The most powerful guerrilla group—the [People's Mujahedin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's_Mujahedin_of_Iran)—was leftist Islamist and opposed the influence of the clergy as reactionary.

Some important clergy did not follow Khomeini's lead. Popular ayatollah [Mahmoud Taleghani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahmoud_Taleghani) supported the left, while perhaps the most senior and influential ayatollah in Iran—[Mohammad Kazem Shariatmadari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammad_Kazem_Shariatmadari)—first remained aloof from politics and then came out in support of a democratic revolution.[^cite_note-108]

Khomeini worked to unite this opposition behind him (except for the unwanted '[atheistic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atheistic) [Marxists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marxists)'),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian1982479-13][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMackey1996276-109] focusing on the [socio-economic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socio-economic) problems of the Shah's government (corruption and unequal income and development),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian1982479-13][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian199330-110] while avoiding specifics among the public that might divide the factions[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian1982478–479-111]—particularly his plan for [clerical rule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hokumat-e_Islami_:_Velayat-e_faqih_\(book_by_Khomeini\)), which he believed most Iranians had become prejudiced against as a result of propaganda campaign by Western [imperialists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imperialist).[^cite_note-112][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKhomeiniAlgar198134-113]

In the post-Shah era, some revolutionaries who clashed with his theocracy and were suppressed by his movement complained of deception,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian199330-110] but in the meantime anti-Shah unity was maintained.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKeddie2003240-114]

### 1970–1977

Several events in the 1970s set the stage for the 1979 revolution.

The 1971 [2,500-year celebration of the Persian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2,500-year_celebration_of_the_Persian_Empire) at [Persepolis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persepolis), organized by the government, was attacked for its extravagance. "As the foreigners reveled on drink forbidden by Islam, Iranians were not only excluded from the festivities, some were starving."[^cite_note-115] Five years later, the Shah angered [pious](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Piety) Iranian Muslims by [changing the first year of the Iranian solar calendar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solar_Hijri_calendar#Iran) from the Islamic *[hijri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hijri_year)* to the ascension to the throne by [Cyrus the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cyrus_the_Great). "Iran jumped overnight from the Muslim year 1355 to the royalist year 2535."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian1982444-116]



The Shah of Iran (left) meeting with members of the US government: [Alfred Atherton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alfred_Atherton), [William Sullivan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_H._Sullivan), [Cyrus Vance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cyrus_Vance), [Jimmy Carter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jimmy_Carter), and [Zbigniew Brzezinski](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zbigniew_Brzezinski), 1977

The [oil boom of the 1970s](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1973_oil_crisis) produced an "alarming" increase in inflation, waste and an "accelerating gap" between the rich and poor, the urban and the rural,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGraham198094-117] along with the presence of tens of thousands of unpopular skilled foreign workers. Many Iranians were also angered by the fact that the Shah's family was the foremost beneficiary of the income generated by oil, and the line between state earnings and family earnings blurred. By 1976, the Shah had accumulated upward of \$1 billion from oil revenue; his family – including 63 princes and princesses had accumulated between \$5 and \$20 billion; and the family foundation controlled approximately \$3 billion.[^cite_note-118] By mid-1977 economic austerity measures to fight inflation disproportionately affected thousands of poor and unskilled male migrants settled in the cities and working in the construction industry. Culturally and religiously conservative,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMoin2000163-119] many went on to form the core of the revolution's demonstrators and "martyrs".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGraham1980226-120]

All Iranians were required to join and pay dues to a new political party, the [Rastakhiz Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rastakhiz_Party) —all other parties were banned.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMoin2000174-121] That party's attempt to fight inflation with populist "anti-[profiteering](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Profiteering)" campaigns—fining and jailing merchants for high prices – angered and politicized merchants while fueling [black markets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_market).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGraham198096-122]

In 1977 the Shah responded to the "polite reminder" of the importance of political rights by the new American president, [Jimmy Carter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jimmy_Carter), by granting amnesty to some prisoners and allowing the [Red Cross](15487.md) to visit prisons. Through 1977 liberal opposition formed organizations and issued open letters denouncing the government.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian1982501–503-123] Against this background a first crucial manifestation of public expression of social discontent and political protest against the regime took place in October 1977, when the German-Iranian Cultural Association in Tehran hosted a series of literature reading sessions, organized by the newly revived Iranian Writers Association and the German [Goethe-Institute](470336.md). In these "Ten Nights" (Dah Shab) 57 of Iran's most prominent poets and writers read their works to thousands of listeners. They demanded the end of censorship and claimed the freedom of expression.[^cite_note-124]

Also in 1977, the popular and influential modernist Islamist theorist [Ali Shariati](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ali_Shariati) died under mysterious circumstances. This both angered his followers, who considered him a martyr at the hands of SAVAK, and removed a potential revolutionary rival to Khomeini. Finally, in October, Khomeini's son Mostafa died of an alleged heart attack, and his death was also blamed on SAVAK. A subsequent memorial service for Mostafa in Tehran put Khomeini back in the spotlight.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMoin2000184–185-125][^cite_note-FOOTNOTETaheri1985182–183-126]

#### Outbreak

See also: [Island of Stability (speech)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Island_of_Stability_\(speech\))

By 1977, the Shah's policy of political [liberalization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberalization) was underway. Secular opponents of the Shah began to meet in secret to denounce the government.[^cite_note-Ritter-127][^cite_note-Pahlavi_2004-128] Led by the leftist intellectual [Saeed Soltanpour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saeed_Soltanpour), the Iranian Writers Association met at the [Goethe Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Goethe_Institute) in Tehran to read anti-government poetry.[^cite_note-Ritter-127] [Ali Shariati](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ali_Shariati)'s death in the United Kingdom shortly after led to another public demonstration, with the opposition accusing the Shah of murdering him.[^cite_note-Milani-Persians-22][^cite_note-Ritter-127]

The chain of events initiating the revolution began with the death of [Mostafa Khomeini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mostafa_Khomeini), chief aide and eldest son of [Ruhollah Khomeini](26232.md). He mysteriously died at midnight on 23 October 1977 in [Najaf, Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Najaf,_Iraq). SAVAK and the [Iraqi government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraqi_government) declared heart attack as the cause of death, though many attributed his death to SAVAK.[^cite_note-129] Khomeini remained silent after the incident, while in Iran with the spread of the news came a wave of protest and mourning ceremonies in several cities.[^cite_note-130][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrumberg2001\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidaPT9QRtDMr8CpgPA92_92\]-131] The mourning of Mostafa was given a political cast by Khomeini's political credentials, their enduring opposition to the monarchy and their exile. This dimension of the ceremonies went beyond the religious credentials of the family.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKurzman2004-27]

## Prelude (1978)

### Beginning of protests (January)

Main article: [1978 Qom protest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1978_Qom_protest)

On 7 January 1978, an article titled "[Iran and Red and Black Colonization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran_and_Red_and_Black_Colonization)" appeared in the national daily *[Ettela'at](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ettela'at)* newspaper. Written under a pseudonym by a government agent, it denounced Khomeini as a "British agent" and a "mad Indian poet" conspiring to sell out Iran to [neo-colonialists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neo-colonialists) and [communists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communists).[^cite_note-Milani_Shah-21][^cite_note-Milani-Persians-22]

The developments initiated by seminaries in the city of Qom closing on 7 January 1978 were followed by the bazaar and seminary closing, and students rallied towards the homes of the religious leaders the next day.[^cite_note-Kurz-132] On 9 January 1978, seminary students and other people demonstrated in the city, and the [Shah](20065598.md)'s security forces cracked down on the demonstrators. The security forces shot live ammunition to disperse the crowd when the peaceful demonstration turned violent.[^cite_note-133] Reports of between 5 and 300 demonstrators were killed in the assault on the protesters

.[^cite_note-Kurz-132][^cite_note-\:0-7]<sup>: 206 </sup> 9 January 1978 (19 Dey) is regarded as a bloody day in Qom.[^cite_note-134][^cite_note-135]

### Consolidation of the opposition (February–March)

See also: [1978 Tabriz protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1978_Tabriz_protests)

According to [Shia](26961.md) custom, memorial services (*chehelom*) are held 40 days after a person's death.[^cite_note-Axworthy-136] Encouraged by Khomeini (who declared that the blood of [martyrs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martyr) must water the "tree of Islam"),[^cite_note-Pahlavi_2004-128] radicals pressured the mosques and moderate clergy to commemorate the deaths of the students, and used the occasion to generate protests.[^cite_note-Rubin-137] The informal network of mosques and bazaars, which for years had been used to carry out religious events, increasingly became consolidated as a coordinated protest organization.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKurzman2004-27][^cite_note-Axworthy-136][^cite_note-Jervis-138]

On 18 February, 40 days after the Qom protests, demonstrations broke out in various cities.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian1982\[\[Category\:Wikipedia_articles_needing_page_number_citations_from_January_2022\]\]<sup_class="noprint_Inline-Template_"_style="white-space\:nowrap;">\[<i>\[\[Wikipedia\:Citing_sources|<span_title="This_citation_requires_a_reference_to_the_specific_page_or_range_of_pages_in_which_the_material_appears. (January_2022)">page needed</span>\]\]</i>\]</sup>-139] [The largest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1978_Tabriz_protests) was in [Tabriz](439791.md), which descended into a full-scale riot. "Western" and government symbols such as cinemas, bars, [state-owned banks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_bank), and police stations were set ablaze.[^cite_note-Axworthy-136] Units of the [Imperial Iranian Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imperial_Iranian_Army) were deployed to restore order. The death toll, according to the government was six,[^cite_note-Eisenstadt-140] while Khomeini claimed hundreds were "martyred".[^cite_note-Afkhami-14][^cite_note-Ritter-127][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian1982510,_512,_513-141]

Forty days later, on 29 March, demonstrations were organized in at least 55 cities, including [Tehran](57654.md).[^cite_note-Axworthy-136] In an increasingly predictable pattern, deadly riots broke out in major cities,[^cite_note-Axworthy-136][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarney1998-142] and again 40 days later, on 10 May. Army [commandos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commando) opened fire on [Shariatmadari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammad_Kazem_Shariatmadari)'s house, killing one of his students. Shariatmadari immediately made a public announcement declaring his support for a "constitutional government" and a return to the policies of the [1906 Constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran_Constitution_of_1906).[^cite_note-Afkhami-14][^cite_note-Pahlavi_2004-128][^cite_note-Axworthy-136]

### Government reaction



Pro-Shah demonstration organized by the [Resurgence Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Resurgence_Party) in [Tabriz](439791.md), April 1978

The Shah was taken completely by surprise by the protests[^cite_note-Afkhami-14][^cite_note-Amuzegar-28] and often became indecisive during times of crisis.[^cite_note-Milani_Shah-21] Virtually every major decision he made backfired and further inflamed the revolutionaries.[^cite_note-Milani_Shah-21]

The Shah decided to continue on his plan of [liberalization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberalization) and to negotiate rather than to use force against the still-nascent protest movement.[^cite_note-Axworthy-136][^cite_note-Rubin-137][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarney1998-142] He promised fully [democratic elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_election) for the [Majlis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Majlis) would be held in 1979, relaxed censorship, and a resolution was drafted to help reduce corruption within the royal family and the government.[^cite_note-Kraft-New_Yorker-143] Protesters were tried in civilian courts rather than by military [courts-martial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Courts-martial) and were quickly released.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian1982\[\[Category\:Wikipedia_articles_needing_page_number_citations_from_January_2022\]\]<sup_class="noprint_Inline-Template_"_style="white-space\:nowrap;">\[<i>\[\[Wikipedia\:Citing_sources|<span_title="This_citation_requires_a_reference_to_the_specific_page_or_range_of_pages_in_which_the_material_appears. (January_2022)">page needed</span>\]\]</i>\]</sup>-139][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarney1998-142]

Iran's security forces had neither received [riot-control](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Riot_control) training nor equipment since 1963.[^cite_note-Eisenstadt-140] As a result, police were unable to control demonstrations, thus the army was frequently deployed.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarney1998-142] Soldiers were instructed not to use [deadly force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deadly_force), yet there were instances of inexperienced soldiers reacting excessively, inflaming the violence without [cowing](494675.md) the opposition, and receiving official condemnation from the Shah.[^cite_note-Eisenstadt-140] The [Carter administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carter_administration) in the US also refused to sell [non-lethal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-lethal) [tear gas](1202646.md) and [rubber bullets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rubber_bullets) to Iran.[^cite_note-Pahlavi_2004-128][^cite_note-Hayward-144]

As early as the February riots in [Tabriz](439791.md), the Shah fired all SAVAK officials in the city as a [concession](2538448.md) to the opposition, and soon began to dismiss civil servants and government officials whom he felt the public blamed.[^cite_note-Afkhami-14][^cite_note-Amuzegar-28][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarney1998-142] In the first national concession, he replaced the hardline SAVAK chief General [Nematollah Nassiri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nematollah_Nassiri) with the more moderate General [Nasser Moghaddam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nasser_Moghaddam).[^cite_note-Milani_Shah-21][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarney1998-142] The government also negotiated with moderate religious leaders such as [Shariatmadari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammad_Kazem_Shariatmadari), apologizing to him for the raid on his house.[^cite_note-Milani-Persians-22]

#### Early summer (June)

By summer, the protests remained at a steady rate for four months, with about 10,000 participants in each major city—with the exception of [Isfahan](223149.md), where protests were larger, and [Tehran](57654.md), where they were smaller—protesting every 40 days. This amounted to a small minority of the more than 15 million adults in Iran.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKurzman2004117-145]

Against the wishes of Khomeini, Shariatmadari called for 17 June [mourning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mourning) protests to be carried out as a one-day stay.[^cite_note-Axworthy-136] Although tension remained, the Shah's policy appeared to have worked, leading Amuzegar to declare that "the crisis is over." A [CIA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CIA) analysis in August concluded that Iran "is not in a revolutionary or even a pre-revolutionary situation."[^cite_note-Carter,_Jimmy_1982,_p.438-146] These and later events in Iran are frequently cited as one of the most consequential strategic surprises that the United States has experienced since the CIA was established in 1947.[^cite_note-147]

As a sign of easing of government restrictions, three prominent opposition leaders from the secular [National Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Front_\(Iran\))—[Karim Sanjabi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karim_Sanjabi), [Shapour Bakhtiar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shapour_Bakhtiar), and [Dariush Forouhar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dariush_Forouhar)—were allowed to pen an open letter to the Shah demanding that he reign according to the [constitution of Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Iran).[^cite_note-Afkhami-14][^cite_note-Pahlavi_2004-128][^cite_note-Kraft-New_Yorker-143]

### Renewed protests (August–September)

#### Appointment of Jafar Sharif-Emami as prime minister (11 August)

Main article: [Cabinet of Jafar Sharif-Emami (1978)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cabinet_of_Jafar_Sharif-Emami_\(1978\))

By August, the protests had "kick\[ed]...into high gear,"[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKurzman200461-148] and the number of demonstrators mushroomed to hundreds of thousands.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKurzman2004117-145] In an attempt to dampen inflation, the [Amuzegar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jamshid_Amouzegar) administration cut spending and reduced business. However, the cutbacks led to a sharp rise in layoffs—particularly among young, unskilled, male workers living in the working-class districts. By summer 1978, the working class joined the street protests in massive numbers.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian1982510,_512,_513-141] In addition, it was the Islamic holy month of [Ramadan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ramadan), bringing a sense of increased religiosity.[^cite_note-Axworthy-136]

A series of escalating protests broke out in major cities, and deadly riots broke out in [Isfahan](223149.md) where protesters fought for the release of [Ayatollah Jalaluddin Taheri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayatollah_Jalaluddin_Taheri).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian1982515-149][^cite_note-Axworthy-136] [Martial law](50477.md) was declared in the city on 11 August as symbols of [Western culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_culture) and government buildings were burned, and a bus full of American workers was bombed.[^cite_note-Axworthy-136][^cite_note-Kraft-New_Yorker-143] Due to his failure to stop the protests, prime minister Amuzegar offered his resignation.

The Shah increasingly felt that he was losing control of the situation and sought to regain it through complete [appeasement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Appeasement).[^cite_note-Afkhami-14][^cite_note-Pahlavi_2004-128] He decided to appoint [Jafar Sharif-Emami](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jafar_Sharif-Emami) to the post of prime minister, himself a veteran prime minister. Emami was chosen due to his family ties to the clergy, although he had a reputation of corruption during his previous premiership.[^cite_note-Milani_Shah-21][^cite_note-Milani-Persians-22]

Under the Shah's guidance, Sharif-Emami effectively began a policy of "appeasing the opposition's demands before they even made them."[^cite_note-Milani-Persians-22] The government abolished the [Rastakhiz Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rastakhiz_Party), legalized all political parties and released political prisoners, increased freedom of expression, curtailed SAVAK's authority and dismissed 34 of its commanders,[^cite_note-Kraft-New_Yorker-143] closed down casinos and nightclubs, and abolished the imperial calendar. The government also began to prosecute corrupt government and royal family members. Sharif-Emami entered into negotiations with Shariatmadari and National Front leader [Karim Sanjabi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karim_Sanjabi) to help organize future elections.[^cite_note-Kraft-New_Yorker-143] Censorship was effectively terminated, and the newspapers began reporting heavily on demonstrations, often highly critically and negatively of the Shah. The [Majlis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Majlis) (parliament) also began issuing resolutions against the government.[^cite_note-Milani_Shah-21]

#### Cinema Rex fire (19 August)

Main article: [Cinema Rex fire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinema_Rex_fire)

On 19 August, in the southwestern city of [Abadan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abadan), four arsonists barred the door of the Cinema Rex movie theatre and set it on fire. In what would be the [largest terrorist attack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_terrorist_incidents) in history prior to the [September 11 attacks](5058690.md) in the US in 2001,[^cite_note-Byman-150] 422 people inside the theatre were [burned to death](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Burned_to_death). Khomeini immediately blamed the Shah and SAVAK for setting the fire, and,[^cite_note-Afkhami-14][^cite_note-Pahlavi_2004-128][^cite_note-Ganji-151] due to the pervasive revolutionary atmosphere, the public also blamed the Shah for starting the fire, despite the government's insistence that they were uninvolved. Tens of thousands of people took to the streets shouting "Burn the Shah!" and "The Shah is the guilty one!"[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian1982\[\[Category\:Wikipedia_articles_needing_page_number_citations_from_January_2022\]\]<sup_class="noprint_Inline-Template_"_style="white-space\:nowrap;">\[<i>\[\[Wikipedia\:Citing_sources|<span_title="This_citation_requires_a_reference_to_the_specific_page_or_range_of_pages_in_which_the_material_appears. (January_2022)">page needed</span>\]\]</i>\]</sup>-139]

After the revolution, many claimed that [Islamist militants](566821.md) had started the fire.[^cite_note-Byman-150][^cite_note-Afkhami,_R._Gholam_2009_page_465_&_459-152][^cite_note-Ansari,_M._Ali_2007_page_259-153][^cite_note-Federal_Research_Division_2004_page_78-154][^cite_note-Bahl,_Taru_2003_page_105-155][^cite_note-Glenn_Eldon_Curtis_2008_page_48-156] After the [Islamic republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_republic) government executed a police officer for the act, a man claiming to be the lone surviving arsonist claimed he was responsible for starting the fire.[^cite_note-iranrights.org-157] After forcing the resignation of the presiding judges in an attempt to hamper the investigation, the new government finally executed Hossein Talakhzadeh for "setting the fire on the Shah's orders," despite his insistence that he did it on his own accord as an ultimate sacrifice for the revolutionary cause.[^cite_note-Afkhami,_R._Gholam_2009_page_465_&_459-152][^cite_note-iranrights.org-157]

#### Declaration of martial law and the Jaleh Square Massacre (8 September)

Main article: [Jaleh Square massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jaleh_Square_massacre)



Demonstration of 8 September 1978. The placard reads: *We want an Islamic government, led by Imam Khomeini*.



Demonstration of "Black Friday" (8 September 1978)

The 4th of September marked [Eid al-Fitr](154944.md), the holiday celebrating the end of [Ramadan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ramadan). A permit for an [open-air](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Open-air_preaching) prayer was granted, in which 200,000–500,000 people attended.[^cite_note-Axworthy-136] Instead, the clergy directed the crowd on a march through the center of Tehran. The Shah reportedly watched from his helicopter, unnerved and confused.[^cite_note-Axworthy-136] A few days later, even larger protests took place, and, for the first time, protesters called for [Khomeini's return](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khomeini's_return) and the establishment of an [Islamic republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_republic).[^cite_note-Axworthy-136]

At midnight on 8 September, the Shah declared [martial law](50477.md) in Tehran and 11 other major cities. All street demonstrations were banned and a [curfew](297681.md) was imposed. Tehran's martial law commander, [general Gholam-Ali Oveissi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gholam_Ali_Oveissi), was known for his severity against opponents.[^cite_note-Afkhami-14][^cite_note-Milani_Shah-21][^cite_note-Milani-Persians-22][^cite_note-Ritter-127][^cite_note-Pahlavi_2004-128][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian1982510,_512,_513-141][^cite_note-Byman-150] However, the Shah made clear that once martial law was lifted, he intended to resume liberalization. He retained Sharif-Emami's civilian government, hoping protesters would avoid taking the streets.[^cite_note-Pahlavi_2004-128][^cite_note-Rubin-137][^cite_note-Kraft-New_Yorker-143]

However, 6,000 protesters took to the streets, either in defiance or because they had missed hearing the declaration, and faced off with soldiers at Jaleh Square.[^cite_note-Afkhami-14][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKurzman2004-27][^cite_note-Pahlavi_2004-128] After [warning shots](1732905.md) failed to disperse the crowd, troops fired directly into it, killing 64.[^cite_note-Axworthy-136] General Oveissi claimed that 30 soldiers were killed by snipers in surrounding buildings.[^cite_note-Afkhami-14][^cite_note-Milani-Persians-22][^cite_note-Amuzegar-28][^cite_note-Pahlavi_2004-128][^cite_note-Axworthy-136][^cite_note-Jervis-138][^cite_note-Ganji-151] Additional clashes throughout the day, which would be called **Black Friday** by the opposition, brought the opposition death toll to 89.[^cite_note-Milani_Shah-21][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian1982510,_512,_513-141][^cite_note-\:0-7]<sup>: 214 </sup>

##### Reactions to Black Friday



Victims of [Black Friday](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Friday_\(1978\))

The deaths shocked the country and damaged any attempt at reconciliation between the Shah and the opposition. Khomeini immediately declared that 4,000 innocent protesters were massacred by "[Zionists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zionists)". Both claims were fabricated- the Israelis had nothing to do with the deaths, and the number killed was 88, not 4000.[^cite_note-158] The claims were believed by many, which gave Khomeini a pretext to reject any further compromise with the government.

The Shah was horrified by [Black Friday](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Friday_\(1978\)) and harshly criticized the events, though this did little to sway public perception of him as being responsible for the shooting, despite Khomeini blaming the "Zionists".[^cite_note-Milani_Shah-21][^cite_note-Axworthy-136][^cite_note-Eisenstadt-140] While martial law officially remained in effect, the government decided not to break up any more demonstrations or strikes (in effect, "martial law without there exactly being martial law," according to Sharif-Emami), instead continuing to negotiate with protest leaders.[^cite_note-Kraft-New_Yorker-143] Consequently, protests often took place without any serious intervention by soldiers.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarney1998-142]

### Nationwide strikes (September–November)

Oil strikes started in the fall of 1978 and led to a 4.8 million barrel drop of crude oil daily production, which was about 7% of the world's supply. Prices rose from 13 dollars per barrel in 1979 to 34 dollars in 1980.[^cite_note-159] While increased production from other countries, like Saudi Arabia, offset the decline in supply slightly there was still a 10% drop in available oil.

There were a variety of factors influencing the workers' decision to strike. The Shah's regime had relaxed oppression in 1977, giving people more access to protest and organizing. At the same time, the economy had faltered after doing better in the early 1970s. The strikes began when the workers at a refinery in Tehran, calling for higher wages and housing allowances, were ignored by the government. The Shah's government responded by killing dozens of strikers in an event known as Black Friday.[^cite_note-Painter-2023-160] The following day, strikes emerged in Tehran, Abadan, Isfahan, Shiraz, and Kermanshah.[^cite_note-161] This first wave of strikes was reported by SAVAK to involve 11,000 workers. The oil strikes of 1978 were imperative to the Iranian Revolution's success, as they put extreme economic pressure on the Shah's regime.[^cite_note-Painter-2023-160] Oil revenues are a significant part of the government's income and regardless of the motivation behind those who strike, oil strikes often threaten to destabilize Iranian regimes.[^cite_note-162]

By late October, a nationwide [general strike](41138909.md) was declared, with workers in virtually all major industries walking off their jobs, most damagingly in the oil industry and the print media.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKurzman2004-27][^cite_note-Ritter-127] Special "strike committees" were set up throughout major industries to organize and coordinate the activities.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian1982515-149]

The Shah did not attempt to crack down on strikers,[^cite_note-Kraft-New_Yorker-143] instead granting them generous wage increases and allowing strikers who lived in government housing to remain in their homes.[^cite_note-Afkhami-14][^cite_note-Milani_Shah-21][^cite_note-Kraft-New_Yorker-143] By early November, many government officials demanded the Shah use forceful measures to bring the strikers back to work.[^cite_note-Afkhami-14][^cite_note-Milani_Shah-21][^cite_note-Ritter-127]

### Khomeini moves to France (November)

Seeking to break Khomeini's contacts with the opposition, the Shah pressured the [Iraqi government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraqi_government) to expel him from [Najaf](200512.md). Khomeini left [Iraq](7515928.md), instead moving to a house bought by Iranian exiles in [Neauphle-le-Château](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neauphle-le-Ch%C3%A2teau), a village near [Paris](22989.md), France. The Shah wished that Khomeini would be cut off from the mosques of Najaf and the protest movement. Instead, the plan backfired badly. French telephone and postal connections, superior to those of Iraq, let Khomeini's supporters flood Iran with tapes and recordings of his sermons.[^cite_note-Milani-Persians-22][^cite_note-Pahlavi_2004-128][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarney1998-142]



Ayatollah Khomeini in Neauphle-le-Château surrounded by journalists

Worse for the Shah was that the [Western media](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_media), especially the [British Broadcasting Corporation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Broadcasting_Corporation) (BBC), immediately put Khomeini into the spotlight.[^cite_note-Milani-Persians-22][^cite_note-163] Khomeini rapidly became a household name in the West, portraying himself as an "Eastern mystic" who did not seek power, but instead sought to "free" his people from "[oppression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oppression)." Western media outlets, usually critical of such claims, became one of Khomeini's most powerful tools.[^cite_note-Milani-Persians-22][^cite_note-Pahlavi_2004-128]

In addition, the media coverage eroded the influence of other, more moderate clergy such as [Ayatollah Shariatmadari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayatollah_Shariatmadari) and [Ayatollah Taleghani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayatollah_Taleghani).[^cite_note-Axworthy-136][^cite_note-Kraft-New_Yorker-143][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarney1998-142] The BBC itself later issued a statement admitting to having a "critical" disposition to the Shah, saying that its broadcasts helped to "change the collective perception of the population."[^cite_note-Milani_Shah-21]

In November, secular [National Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Front_\(Iran\)) leader [Karim Sanjabi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karim_Sanjabi) flew to Paris to meet Khomeini. There the two signed an agreement for a draft constitution that would be "Islamic and democratic". It signaled the now official alliance between the clergy and the secular opposition.[^cite_note-Milani_Shah-21][^cite_note-Axworthy-136] To help create a democratic façade, Khomeini placed [Westernized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Westernized) figures (such as [Sadegh Ghotbzadeh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sadegh_Ghotbzadeh) and [Ebrahim Yazdi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ebrahim_Yazdi)) as the public spokesmen of the opposition, and never spoke to the media of his intentions to create a [theocracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theocracy).[^cite_note-Milani_Shah-21]

### University of Tehran protest (5 November)

Street demonstrations continued at full force with little response from the military; by late October, government officials effectively ceded the [University of Tehran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Tehran) to student protesters.[^cite_note-Kraft-New_Yorker-143][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarney1998-142] Worse, the opposition was increasingly armed, firing at soldiers and attacking banks and government buildings in an attempt to destabilize the country.[^cite_note-Amuzegar-28][^cite_note-Pahlavi_2004-128]

On 5 November, demonstrations at University of Tehran became deadly after a fight broke out with armed soldiers.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian1982515-149][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKurzman2004-27][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarney1998-142] Within hours, [Tehran](57654.md) broke out into a full-scale riot. Block after block of Western symbols such as movie theaters and department stores, as well as government and police buildings, were seized, looted, and burned. The [British embassy in Tehran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_embassy_in_Tehran) was partially burned and vandalized; the [American embassy](24966434.md) nearly suffered the same fate. The event became known to foreign observers as "The Day Tehran Burned."[^cite_note-Afkhami-14][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarney1998-142][^cite_note-Brotons-164]

Many rioters were young teenage boys, often organized by the mosques in southern Tehran and encouraged by their [mullahs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mullah) to attack and destroy western and secular symbols.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKurzman2004-27][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarney1998-142][^cite_note-Brotons-164] The army and police, confused about their orders and under pressure from the Shah not to risk initiating violence, effectively gave up and did not intervene.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarney1998-142][^cite_note-Brotons-164][^cite_note-Zabir-165]

### Appointment of a military government (6 November)

Main article: [Military government of Gholam-Reza Azhari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_government_of_Gholam-Reza_Azhari)

As the situation on the streets spiraled out of control, many well known and reputable figures within the country began to approach the Shah, begging him to stop the chaos.[^cite_note-Milani_Shah-21][^cite_note-Amuzegar-28][^cite_note-Pahlavi_2004-128][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarney1998-142]

On 6 November, the Shah dismissed Sharif-Emami from the post of prime minister, appointing a military government in its place.[^cite_note-Milani_Shah-21][^cite_note-Brotons-164] The Shah chose general [Gholam-Reza Azhari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gholam-Reza_Azhari) to be prime minister because of his mild-mannered approach to the situation.[^cite_note-Afkhami-14][^cite_note-Pahlavi_2004-128][^cite_note-Brotons-164] The cabinet he would choose was a military cabinet in name only and consisted primarily of civilian leaders.[^cite_note-Brotons-164]

The same day, the Shah made a speech on Iranian television.[^cite_note-Milani_Shah-21][^cite_note-Milani-Persians-22][^cite_note-Zabir-165] He referred to himself as [*Padeshah*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Padishah) ('master king'), instead of the more grandiose [Shahanshah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shahanshah) (king of kings), which he insisted on being called previously.[^cite_note-Kraft-New_Yorker-143] In his speech he stated "I have heard the voice of your revolution ... this revolution cannot but be supported by me, the king of Iran".[^cite_note-Kraft-New_Yorker-143][^cite_note-Majd-166] He apologized for mistakes that were committed during his reign, and promised to ensure that corruption would no longer exist.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarney1998-142][^cite_note-Zabir-165] He stated he would begin to work with the opposition to bring democracy, and would form a coalition government.[^cite_note-Afkhami-14][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarney1998-142][^cite_note-Zabir-165] In effect, the Shah intended to restrain the military government (which he described as a temporary caretaker government) from carrying out a full crackdown.[^cite_note-Kraft-New_Yorker-143]

The speech backfired when the revolutionaries sensed weakness from the Shah and "smelled blood."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarney1998-142][^cite_note-Majd-166] Khomeini announced that there would be no reconciliation with the Shah and called on all Iranians to overthrow him.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarney1998-142][^cite_note-Majd-166]

Military authorities declared martial law in [Khuzestan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khuzestan) province (Iran's main oil producing province) and deployed troops to its oil facilities. Navy personnel were also used as [strikebreakers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strikebreakers) in the oil industry.[^cite_note-Afkhami-14][^cite_note-Pahlavi_2004-128][^cite_note-Brotons-164] Street marches declined, and oil production began increasing once again, nearly reaching pre-revolutionary levels.[^cite_note-Pahlavi_2004-128][^cite_note-Brotons-164] In a symbolic blow to the opposition, [Karim Sanjabi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karim_Sanjabi), who had visited Khomeini in Paris, was arrested upon his return to Iran.[^cite_note-Kraft-New_Yorker-143]

However, the government still continued the policy of [appeasement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Appeasement) and negotiation.[^cite_note-Milani_Shah-21][^cite_note-Milani-Persians-22][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarney1998-142][^cite_note-Zabir-165] The Shah ordered the arrest of 100 officials from his own government for charges of corruption, including former prime minister [Amir Abbas-Hoveyda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amir_Abbas_Hoveida) and former SAVAK head [Nematollah Nassiri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nematollah_Nassiri).[^cite_note-Milani_Shah-21][^cite_note-Milani-Persians-22][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarney1998-142]

### Muharram protests (early December)



[Mohammad Beheshti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammad_Beheshti) in the Tehran Ashura demonstration, 11 December 1978

People marching during the Iranian Revolution, 1979

Khomeini condemned the military government and called for continued protests.[^cite_note-Axworthy-136][^cite_note-news.google.com-167] He and the protest organizers planned a series of escalating protests during the holy Islamic month of [Muharram](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muharram), to culminate with massive protests on the days of [Tasu'a](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tasu'a) and [Ashura](488563.md), the latter commemorating the [martyrdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Karbala) of [Imam Husayn ibn Ali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imam_Husayn_ibn_Ali), the third Shia Muslim [imam](15240.md).[^cite_note-Axworthy-136]

While the military authorities banned street demonstrations and extended the curfew, the Shah faced deep misgivings about the potential violence.[^cite_note-Kraft-New_Yorker-143]

On 2 December 1978, the Muharram protests began. Named for the Islamic month in which they began, the Muharram protests were huge and pivotal. Over two million protesters[^cite_note-Muharram_Protests_in_Iran,_1978-168] (many teenagers proselytized by [mullahs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mullah) from the mosques of southern Tehran) took to the streets, crowding [Shahyad Square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Azadi_Square). Protesters frequently went out at night, violating the curfew, often taking to rooftops and shouting "[*Allahu-akbar*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allahu_akbar)" ('God is great'). According to one witness, many of the clashes on the street had an air of playfulness, with security forces using "kid gloves" against the opposition.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarney1998-142] Nevertheless, the government reported at least 12 opposition deaths.[^cite_note-news.google.com-167]

The protesters demanded that Shah [Mohammad Reza Pahlavi](20065598.md) step down from power and that [Grand Ayatollah Ruhollah Khomeini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Ayatollah_Ruhollah_Khomeini) be returned from exile. The protests grew incredibly fast, reaching between six million and nine million in strength in the first week. About 5% of the population had taken to the streets in the Muharram protests. Both beginning and ending in the month of Muharram, the protests succeeded, and the Shah stepped down from power later that month.[^cite_note-Muharram_Protests_in_Iran,_1978-168]

After the success of what would become known as a revolution, Ayatollah [Khomeini returned to Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khomeini_returned_to_Iran) as its religious and political leader for life. Khomeini had been an opposition leader to Shah for many years, rising to prominence after the death of his mentor, renowned scholar [Yazdi Ha'iri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yazdi_Ha'iri), in the 1930s.[^cite_note-Ayatollah_Ruhollah_Khomeini-169] Even in his years in exile, Khomeini remained relevant in Iran. Supporting the protests from beyond Iran's borders, he proclaimed that "freedom and liberation from the bonds of imperialism" was imminent.[^cite_note-Ayatollah_Ruhollah_Khomeini-169]

#### Tasu'a and Ashura marches (10–11 December)





Tehran Ashura demonstration, 11 December 1978

As the days of [Tasu'a](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tasu'a) and [Ashura](488563.md) (10 and 11 December) approached, to prevent a deadly showdown, the Shah began to draw back. In negotiations with [Ayatollah Shariatmadari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayatollah_Shariatmadari), the Shah ordered the release of 120 political prisoners and [Karim Sanjabi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karim_Sanjabi), and on 8 December revoked the ban on street demonstrations. Permits were issued for the marchers, and troops were removed from the procession's path. In turn, Shariatmadari pledged that to make sure that there would be no violence during the demonstrations.[^cite_note-Kraft-New_Yorker-143]

On 10 and 11 December 1978, the days of Tasu'a and Ashura, between 6 and 9 million anti-Shah demonstrators marched throughout Iran. According to one historian, "even discounting for exaggeration, these figures may represent the largest protest event in history."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKurzman2004122-170] The marches were led by Ayatollah [Taleghani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taleghani) and National Front leader Karim Sanjabi, thus symbolizing the "unity" of the secular and religious opposition. The [mullahs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mullah) and [bazaaris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bazaari) effectively policed the gathering, and protesters who attempted to initiate violence were restrained.[^cite_note-Axworthy-136]

More than 10% of the country marched in anti-Shah demonstrations on the two days, possibly a higher percentage than any previous revolution.
