It is rare for a revolution to involve as much as 1% of a country's population; the French, Russian, and Romanian revolutions may have passed the 1% mark.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKurzman2004121-35]

## Revolution (late 1978–1979)

Much of Iranian society was in [euphoria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Euphoria) about the coming revolution. Secular and leftist politicians piled onto the movement seeking to gain power in the aftermath, ignoring the fact that Khomeini was the very antithesis to all of the positions they supported.[^cite_note-Milani_Shah-21] While it was increasingly clear to more secular Iranians that Khomeini was not a liberal, he was widely perceived as a figurehead, and that power would eventually be handed to the secular groups.[^cite_note-Milani_Shah-21][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarney1998-142]

### Demoralization of the army (December, 1978)



A protester giving flowers to an army officer

The military leadership was increasingly paralyzed by indecision, and rank-and-file soldiers were demoralized, having been forced to confront demonstrators while prohibited from using their own weapons (and being condemned by the Shah if they did).[^cite_note-Eisenstadt-140] Increasingly, Khomeini called on the soldiers of the armed forces to defect to the opposition.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian1982\[\[Category\:Wikipedia_articles_needing_page_number_citations_from_January_2022\]\]<sup_class="noprint_Inline-Template_"_style="white-space\:nowrap;">\[<i>\[\[Wikipedia\:Citing_sources|<span_title="This_citation_requires_a_reference_to_the_specific_page_or_range_of_pages_in_which_the_material_appears. (January_2022)">page needed</span>\]\]</i>\]</sup>-139][^cite_note-Pahlavi_2004-128] Revolutionaries gave flowers and civilian clothes to deserters, while threatening retribution to those who stayed.

On 11 December, a dozen officers were shot dead by their own troops at Tehran's [Lavizan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lavizan) barracks. Fearing further [mutinies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mutinies), many soldiers were returned to their barracks.[^cite_note-Eisenstadt-140] [Mashhad](406512.md) (the second largest city in Iran) was abandoned to the protesters, and in many provincial towns demonstrators were effectively in control.[^cite_note-Axworthy-136]

### American and internal negotiations with the opposition (late December, 1978)

Main article: [Jimmy Carter's engagement with Ruhollah Khomeini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jimmy_Carter's_engagement_with_Ruhollah_Khomeini)

The [Carter administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carter_administration) increasingly became locked in a debate about continued support for the monarchy.[^cite_note-Little-171] As early as November, ambassador William Sullivan sent a telegram to Carter (the "[Thinking the Unthinkable](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thinking_the_Unthinkable?action=edit\&redlink=1)" telegram[^cite_note-Little-171]). The telegram effectively declared his belief that the Shah would not survive the protests and that the US should consider withdrawing its support for his government and persuading the monarch to abdicate. The United States would then help assemble a coalition of pro-Western military officers, middle class professionals, and moderate clergy, with Khomeini installed as a [Gandhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gandhi)-like spiritual leader.[^cite_note-Little-171]

The telegram touched off a vigorous debate in the American cabinet, with some, such as National Security Advisor [Zbigniew Brzezinski](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zbigniew_Brzezinski),[^cite_note-Little-171] rejecting it outright. Secretary of State [Cyrus Vance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cyrus_Vance) rejected a military crackdown;[^cite_note-Axworthy-136] he and his supporters believed in the "moderate and progressive" intentions of Khomeini and his circle.[^cite_note-Hayward-144][^cite_note-Little-171]

Increasing contact was established with the pro-Khomeini camp. Based on the revolutionaries' responses, some American officials (especially Ambassador Sullivan) felt Khomeini genuinely intended to create a democracy.[^cite_note-Milani_Shah-21] According to historian [Abbas Milani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abbas_Milani), this led to the United States facilitating Khomeini's rise to power.[^cite_note-Milani_Shah-21][^cite_note-172][^cite_note-173]

The Shah began to search for a new prime minister, one who was a civilian and a member of the opposition. On 28 December, he secured an agreement with another major National Front figure, [Shapour Bakhtiar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shapour_Bakhtiar). Bakhtiar would be appointed prime minister (a return to civilian rule), while the Shah and his family would leave the country. His royal duties would be carried out by a Regency Council, and three months after his departure a referendum would be submitted to the people deciding on whether Iran would remain a monarchy or become a republic. A former opponent of the Shah, Bakhtiar became motivated to join the government because he was increasingly aware of Khomeini's intentions to implement hard-line religious rule rather than a democracy.[^cite_note-Milani-Persians-22] Karim Sanjabi immediately expelled Bakhtiar from the National Front, and Bakhtiar was denounced by Khomeini (who declared that acceptance of his government was the equivalent of "obedience to false gods").[^cite_note-Milani_Shah-21][^cite_note-Geist-174]

### The Shah leaves (16 January 1979)

Main article: [Regency Council (Iran)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regency_Council_\(Iran\))



Shah and his wife, [Shahbanu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shahbanu) [Farah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Farah_Pahlavi), leaving Iran on 16 January 1979



Ayatollah [Khomeini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khomeini) giving a speech after arranging a press-conference at [Neauphle-le-Château](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neauphle-le-Ch%C3%A2teau), France, the day after the departure of the Shah



Front cover of *[Ettela'at](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ettela'at)*, 16 January 1979, featuring the now-famous headline "[The Shah Is Gone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Shah_Is_Gone)". The front cover of the same day's edition of *[Kayhan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kayhan)* featured the same headline

The Shah, wanting to see Bakhtiar established, kept delaying his departure. Consequently, to the Iranian public, Bakhtiar was seen as the Shah's last prime minister, undermining his support.[^cite_note-Axworthy-136]

American general [Robert Huyser](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Huyser), the deputy commander of [US European Command](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/US_European_Command), entered Iran.[^cite_note-Milani_Shah-21] While the option of a pro-Shah military coup still was a possibility, Huyser met with military leaders (but not the Shah) and established meetings between them and Khomeini allies for the purpose of agreeing on Bakhtiar's transitional government.[^cite_note-Milani_Shah-21][^cite_note-Pahlavi_2004-128][^cite_note-Axworthy-136][^cite_note-Huyser-175] Ambassador Sullivan disagreed, and attempted to pressure Huyser to ignore the military and work directly with Khomeini's opposition.[^cite_note-Axworthy-136][^cite_note-Huyser-175] Nevertheless, Huyser won out and continued to work with both the military and opposition. He left Iran on 3 February.[^cite_note-Axworthy-136][^cite_note-Huyser-175] The Shah was privately embittered by Huyser's mission and felt that the United States no longer wanted him in power.[^cite_note-Pahlavi_2004-128]

On the morning of 16 January 1979, Bakhtiar was officially appointed prime minister. The same day, a tearful Shah and his family left Iran for exile in [Egypt](8087628.md), never to return.[^cite_note-Milani_Shah-21]

### Bakhtiar's premiership and Ayatollah Khomeini's return (January–February, 1979)

Main articles: [Ruhollah Khomeini's return to Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ruhollah_Khomeini's_return_to_Iran), [Council of the Islamic Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_the_Islamic_Revolution), and [Interim Government of Iran (1979)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interim_Government_of_Iran_\(1979\))

> *You will see we are not in any particular animosity with the Americans.*\
>
>
> [Ayatollah Khomeini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayatollah_Khomeini) in a [secret message](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jimmy_Carter's_engagement_with_Ruhollah_Khomeini) to the [Carter administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency_of_Jimmy_Carter), January 27th, 1979[^cite_note-176]



Cartoon depicting [Shapour Bakhtiar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shapour_Bakhtiar) and [Mosaddegh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mosaddegh) on 22 January 1978 issue of *[Ettela'at](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ettela'at)*, during the revolution

When the news of the Shah's departure was announced from newspapers and news at 2 pm on Iran's National Radio, there were spontaneous scenes of joy throughout the country. Millions poured onto the streets, and virtually every remaining sign of the monarchy was torn down by the crowds.[^cite_note-Axworthy-136][^cite_note-google-177] Bakhtiar dissolved SAVAK and freed all remaining political prisoners. He ordered the army to allow mass demonstrations, promised free elections and invited the revolutionaries into a government of "national unity".[^cite_note-Geist-174][^cite_note-178]

Video of people welcoming Ayatollah Khomeini in the streets of Tehran after his return from exile

Bakhtiar invited Khomeini back to Iran, with the intention of creating a [Vatican](32408.md)-like state in the holy city of [Qom](638742.md), declaring that "We will soon have the honor of welcoming home the Ayatollah Khomeini".[^cite_note-Geist-174] On 1 February 1979 Khomeini returned to Tehran in a chartered Air France [Boeing 747](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boeing_747).[^cite_note-179] The welcoming crowd of several million Iranians was so large he was forced to take a helicopter after the car taking him from the airport was overwhelmed by enthusiastic supporters.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian2008161-180]

Khomeini was now not only the undisputed leader of the revolution,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGölz2017229-8][^cite_note-FOOTNOTETaheri1985146-181] he had become someone seen as a divinely-appointed figure, greeted as he descended from his airplane with cries of 'Khomeini, O Imam, we salute you, peace be upon you.'[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMoin2000200-182] Crowds were now known to chant "Islam, Islam, Khomeini, We Will Follow You", and even "Khomeini for King."[^cite_note-Foucault_Dreaming-183]

When asked by a reporter how he felt returning to his home country after a long exile, Khomeini replied "Nothing". On the day of his arrival Khomeini made clear his rejection of Bakhtiar's government in a speech promising, "I shall kick their teeth in. I appoint the government, I appoint the government in support of this nation".[^cite_note-Geist-174] On 5 February at his headquarters in the [Refah School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Refah_School) in southern Tehran, he declared a provisional revolutionary government, appointed opposition leader [Mehdi Bazargan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mehdi_Bazargan) (from the religious-nationalist [Freedom Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_Movement_of_Iran), affiliated with the National Front) as his own prime minister, and commanded Iranians to obey Bazargan as a religious duty:[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian1982515-149][^cite_note-Milani-Persians-22][^cite_note-Axworthy-136][^cite_note-Geist-174]

> \[T]hrough the guardianship \[*Velayat*] that I have from the holy lawgiver \[God], I hereby pronounce Bazargan as the Ruler, and since I have appointed him, he must be obeyed. The nation must obey him. This is not an ordinary government. It is a government based on the *sharia*. Opposing this government means opposing the *sharia* of Islam ... Revolt against God's government is a revolt against God. Revolt against God is blasphemy.[^cite_note-184][^cite_note-Khomeini-185]

Angered, Bakhtiar made a speech of his own. Reaffirming himself as the legitimate leader, he declared that:

> Iran has one government. More than this is intolerable, either for me or for you or for any other Iranian. As a Muslim, I had not heard that jihad refers to one Muslim against other Muslims.... I will not give permission to Ayatollah Khomeini to form an interim government. In life there comes a time when one must stand firm and say no.... I have never seen a book about an Islamic republic; neither has anyone else for that matter.... Some of the people surrounding the Ayatollah are like violent vultures.... The clergy should go to Qom and build a wall around themselves and create their own Vatican.[^cite_note-Geist-174]&#x20;

### Armed battles and collapse of the monarchy (February, 1979)

See also: [Homafaran Allegiance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homafaran_Allegiance)

Tensions between the two rival governments increased rapidly. To demonstrate his support, Khomeini called for demonstrators to occupy the streets throughout the country. He also sent a letter to American officials warning them to withdraw support for Bakhtiar.[^cite_note-Milani_Shah-21] Bakhtiar became increasingly isolated, with members of the government (including the entire [Regency Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regency_Council_\(Iran\))) defecting to Khomeini. The military was crumbling, its leadership completely paralyzed, unsure of whether to support Bakhtiar or act on their own, with rank-and-file soldiers either demoralized or deserting.[^cite_note-Axworthy-136][^cite_note-Eisenstadt-140]

On 9 February, a rebellion of pro-Khomeini air force technicians broke out at the [Doshan Tappeh Air Base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doshan_Tappeh_Air_Base). A unit of the pro-Shah [Immortal Guards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imperial_Guard_\(Iran\)) attempted to apprehend the rebels, and an armed battle broke out. Soon large crowds took to the streets, building barricades and supporting the rebels, while Islamic-Marxist guerillas with their weapons joined in support.[^cite_note-Axworthy-136]



Iranian armed rebels during the revolution

The armed rebels attacked a weapons factory, capturing nearly 50,000 machine guns and distributing them to civilians who joined in the fighting. The rebels stormed police stations and military bases throughout Tehran. The city's martial law commander, general [Mehdi Rahimi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mehdi_Rahimi), decided not to use his 30,000 loyal Immortal Guards to crush the rebellion for fear of producing civilian casualties.[^cite_note-Zabir-165]



11 February Army announcement, signed by top generals, proclaiming its political neutrality

The final collapse of the Bakhtiar's government came at 10 am (local time) on 11 February when the Supreme Military Council declared itself "neutral in the current political disputes ... in order to prevent further disorder and bloodshed."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMoin2000206-186][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian1982529-187] All military personnel were ordered back to their bases, effectively yielding control of the entire country to Khomeini.[^cite_note-Eisenstadt-140] Revolutionaries took over government buildings, TV and radio stations, and palaces of the [Pahlavi dynasty](338883.md), marking the end of the monarchy in Iran. Bakhtiar escaped the palace under a hail of bullets, fleeing Iran in disguise.&#x20;

This period, from 1 to 11 February, is celebrated every year in Iran as the "[Decade of Fajr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Decade_of_Fajr)".[^cite_note-188] 11 February is "Islamic Revolution's Victory Day", a national holiday with state sponsored demonstrations in every city.[^cite_note-RFERL-189][^cite_note-190]

### Casualties

Further information: [Casualties of the Iranian Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Casualties_of_the_Iranian_Revolution)

Some sources (such as [Emadeddin Baghi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emadeddin_Baghi), a researcher at the [Martyrs Foundation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bonyad#Islamic_Republic)) claim 2,781 protesters and revolutionaries were killed in 1978–79 during the revolution.[^cite_note-Baghi-191][^cite_note-192] Khomeini reported a much larger number, saying "60,000 men, women and children were martyred by the Shah's regime."[^cite_note-Baqi-2003-193][^cite_note-Baghi-191][^cite_note-194] Military historian [Spencer C. Tucker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spencer_C._Tucker) rejected this 60,000 figure as "grossly overstated ... for propaganda purposes".[^cite_note-Tucker-195] Tucker explains that the consensus of historians regarding estimated deaths during the Iranian Revolution (from January 1978 to February 1979), numbers between 532 and 2,781.[^cite_note-Tucker-195]

According to the historian [Ervand Abrahamian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ervand_Abrahamian), 8,000 opponents were executed between June 1981 and June 1985 by revolutionary courts as the revolution was consolidated exceeded those killed by the royalist government trying to stop the revolution.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbrahamian2008181-196] According to Tucker's estimations, in the period of 1980 to 1985, between 25,000 and 40,000 Iranians were arrested, 15,000 Iranians were tried, and between 8,000 and 9,500 Iranians were executed, although UN sources note that other estimates are far higher.[^cite_note-Tucker-195][^cite_note-Rehman-197] In 2025 the Iranian government created a parking lot by paving over a section of [Behesht-e Zahra cemetery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Behesht-e_Zahra) in Tehran that had held the remains of an estimated 5,000 to 7,000 victims of the mass executions that followed the 1979 revolution. The year before, United Nations Special Rapporteur [Javaid Rehman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Javaid_Rehman) described the regime's pattern of destroying graveyards as an attempt to “conceal or erase data that could serve as potential evidence to avoid legal accountability.”[^cite_note-Gambrell-198][^cite_note-Rehman-197]

### Songs of Iranian Revolution

See also: [We Are Armed with Allahu Akbar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/We_Are_Armed_with_Allahu_Akbar) and [Payandeh Bada Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Payandeh_Bada_Iran)

The songs most closely associated with the revolution are epic ballads, composed during and in support of the Islamic Revolution and in opposition to the Pahlavi dynasty.[^cite_note-Torab2007-199] Before the revolution was consolidated, these chants were made by various political supporters, and were often recorded on cassette tapes in underground and home studios. In schools, these songs were sung by students as part of the celebrations of Fajr Decades.[^cite_note-200] "Iran Iran" or "Allah Allah" chants are famous revolutionary songs.[^cite_note-201]

## Women's role

&#x20;\


Main article: [Women in Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Women_in_Iran)

The Iranian Revolution was a gendered revolution; much of the new regime's rhetoric was centered on the position of women in society.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAzari1983\[httpsarchiveorgdetailswomenofiranconfl0000unsepage150_150\]-202] Beyond rhetoric, thousands of women were also heavily mobilized in the revolution itself,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAzari1983\[httpsarchiveorgdetailswomenofiranconfl0000unsepage151_151\]-203] and different groups of women actively participated alongside their male counterparts.[^cite_note-204] Not only participating through voting, women also contributed to the revolution through marches, demonstrations and chanting slogans.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEIsfandiyārī19975-205] Women were involved in caring for the wounded, including doctors who responded to calls for help and opened their homes for those who needed assistance. While women themselves were often killed, tortured, arrested or injured and some were involved in guerilla activities, most contributed in non-violent ways.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENashat1983\[\[Category\:Wikipedia_articles_needing_page_number_citations_from_January_2022\]\]<sup_class="noprint_Inline-Template_"_style="white-space\:nowrap;">\[<i>\[\[Wikipedia\:Citing_sources|<span_title="This_citation_requires_a_reference_to_the_specific_page_or_range_of_pages_in_which_the_material_appears. (January_2022)">page needed</span>\]\]</i>\]</sup>-206] Many women were instrumental not only in being involved in the revolution themselves but in mobilizing men and other non-political women. Many women protested while carrying children and their presence was one of the main reasons for disarming soldiers (who were there on behalf of the regime) who were ordered to shoot if necessary.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENashat1983\[\[Category\:Wikipedia_articles_needing_page_number_citations_from_January_2022\]\]<sup_class="noprint_Inline-Template_"_style="white-space\:nowrap;">\[<i>\[\[Wikipedia\:Citing_sources|<span_title="This_citation_requires_a_reference_to_the_specific_page_or_range_of_pages_in_which_the_material_appears. (January_2022)">page needed</span>\]\]</i>\]</sup>-206]

### Ayatollah Khomeini's rhetoric on women's participation

[Ayatollah Khomeini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayatollah_Khomeini) asserted that "You ladies here have proved that you are at the forefront of this movement. You have a great share in our Islamic movement. The future of our country depends on your support."[^cite_note-al-islam.org-207] He invoked the image of the [hijab](68301.md) as a symbol of the revolution, saying that "a nation whose respected women demonstrate in modest garb \[*hejab*] to express their disgust with the Shah's regime—such a nation will be victorious."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKurzman2005151-208] He also said that "women from all levels of society took part in the recent demonstrations, which we are calling the 'referendum of the streets'... women fought side by side with men in the struggle for their independence and their liberty."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENashat1983\[httpsarchiveorgdetailswomenrevolutioni0000unsepage116_116\]-209] Khomeini pleaded for women to participate in anti-Shah demonstrations in various cities. Furthermore, women later responded to Khomeini's urgings to vote in favor of the Islamic Republic and the new constitution.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEIsfandiyārī19975-205] Women were so pivotal to the revolution that in response to a suggestion from a top aide to ban women from coming to group audience, Khomeini said "I threw the Shah out with these women, there's no problem in their coming."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKurzman2005151-208]

After the revolution, Khomeini credited much of the success of the movement to women, even commending the women for mobilizing men, "you ladies have proved that you are in the vanguard of the movement, you have proved that you lead the men, men get their inspiration from you, the men of Iran have learnt lessons from the honourable ladies of Iran ... You are in the vanguard of the movement."[^cite_note-al-islam.org-207]

It has been argued that Khomeini and his fellow leaders danced around the issue of women's rights and rather focused their rhetoric on mobilizing women through encouraging them to participate in protests and fueling their anti-Shah sentiments.[^cite_note-Brooks-2008-210]

### Variation within women's participation





The presence of [segregated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sex_segregation_in_Iran) women in the Tehran Ashura Demonstration, 11 December 1978

The motivations of women for being part of the revolutions were complex and varied among a plethora of religious, political and economic reasons[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKurzman2005152-211] and women participating were from various classes and backgrounds.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEIsfandiyārī1997-212] Many Western educated upper-middle-class women from secular, urban and professional families were involved as well as many women from working-class and rural backgrounds.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENashat1983\[\[Category\:Wikipedia_articles_needing_page_number_citations_from_January_2022\]\]<sup_class="noprint_Inline-Template_"_style="white-space\:nowrap;">\[<i>\[\[Wikipedia\:Citing_sources|<span_title="This_citation_requires_a_reference_to_the_specific_page_or_range_of_pages_in_which_the_material_appears. (January_2022)">page needed</span>\]\]</i>\]</sup>-206] There were groups as varied as the [Fida'iyan-i Khalq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fida'iyan-i_Khalq), and the [Mujahedin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's_Mujahedin_of_Iran) were functioning as guerrilla units during the revolutions in opposition to the Shah's regime.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENashat1983\[\[Category\:Wikipedia_articles_needing_page_number_citations_from_January_2022\]\]<sup_class="noprint_Inline-Template_"_style="white-space\:nowrap;">\[<i>\[\[Wikipedia\:Citing_sources|<span_title="This_citation_requires_a_reference_to_the_specific_page_or_range_of_pages_in_which_the_material_appears. (January_2022)">page needed</span>\]\]</i>\]</sup>-206] There were also other groups of women with various agendas that sometimes converged and sometimes diverged from the Islamic Republic's political positions. For example, organized feminism, which was around since the Pahlavi dynasty, joined the revolutionary movement after the Shah dropped the cabinet position on Women's Affairs to appease the Islamists.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKurzman2005151-208] Members of the [Women's Organization of Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Women's_Organization_of_Iran) marched in support for the revolution and it was important that women very much linked to the government also turned against the Shah's regime.[^cite_note-Brooks-2008-210] Yet, there was some tension later between feminists' dress and the revolution's stance on women's clothing and they began to feel uncomfortable at opposition events.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKurzman2005152-211]

Some argue that this politicization and mobilization of women made it difficult for the new regime to push them out of the public and political spheres. The revolution resulted in an unprecedented opening for Iranian women into politics (mostly through demonstrations and voting),[^cite_note-213] and some authors argue that this had a lasting impact on Iranian women's political participation and role in the public sphere.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEIsfandiyārī19975-205] Some women were also part of the inner circle of the leaders of the new regime such as [Marzieh Hadidchi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marzieh_Hadidchi). Other than the politicization of women, there were particular circumstances during the revolution which pushed women into being involved with politics. For example, "the combination of martial law with its curfew hours and the closing down of shops and workplaces, together with the cold of the fall and winter months resulted in the centers of political discussion often being within the home."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENashat1983\[httpsarchiveorgdetailswomenrevolutioni0000unsepage121_121\]-214] Women engaged with news and media as well as political discussions alongside their male counterparts as "the revolution was the only topic of interest to anyone, regardless of age or sex."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENashat1983\[httpsarchiveorgdetailswomenrevolutioni0000unsepage121_121\]-214] During 1978 and 1979 there were many gatherings in women's homes where they exchanged interpersonal news and anecdotes. These personal accounts were valuable in a time when the official coverage of news was not trusted by many people.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENashat1983\[\[Category\:Wikipedia_articles_needing_page_number_citations_from_January_2022\]\]<sup_class="noprint_Inline-Template_"_style="white-space\:nowrap;">\[<i>\[\[Wikipedia\:Citing_sources|<span_title="This_citation_requires_a_reference_to_the_specific_page_or_range_of_pages_in_which_the_material_appears. (January_2022)">page needed</span>\]\]</i>\]</sup>-206]

Women who were activists, religious women and women dissatisfied with the regime were able to unite under the anti-Shah umbrella. However, "women were not united in their opinions of the revolution and its outcome as much as they were not united in their reasons for joining the revolution".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENashat1983\[httpsarchiveorgdetailswomenrevolutioni0000unsepage77_77\]-215] Despite this mobilization and high participation rate of women, they were still kept out of leadership positions which were exclusive to men; women are thought to be part of the rank and file rather than the elite strata of the revolution.[^cite_note-Brooks-2008-210]

### Academic literature on women's participation

While there has been some academic literature exploring individual narratives of women on the revolution,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEIsfandiyārī19975-205] most of the academic work produced focuses on the effect of the revolution on women rather than the role of Iranian women during the revolution. Scholar [Guity Nashat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guity_Nashat?action=edit\&redlink=1) highlights this neglected aspect of the revolution, "Although women's participation in the events leading to the 11 February revolution was instrumental in its success, most studies have not addressed the reasons for their involvement or their contribution."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENashat1983\[httpsarchiveorgdetailswomenrevolutioni0000unsepage1_1\]-216] Janet Baur argues the necessity of examining the daily lives of women, their living conditions and their relationship to other groups to understand their participation in the socio-political events of the revolution. She further explains that the cultural, ideological, social and material factors shaping the social life and class differences in the period just prior to the revolution need to be studied to understand how the Iranian women's social consciousness developed and how it led them to take part in public protests.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENashat1983\[\[Category\:Wikipedia_articles_needing_page_number_citations_from_January_2022\]\]<sup_class="noprint_Inline-Template_"_style="white-space\:nowrap;">\[<i>\[\[Wikipedia\:Citing_sources|<span_title="This_citation_requires_a_reference_to_the_specific_page_or_range_of_pages_in_which_the_material_appears. (January_2022)">page needed</span>\]\]</i>\]</sup>-206] Caroline M. Brooks argues that women were left to express their concerns through the protest rather than in the [Majlis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Majlis_of_Iran). Thus, this created a "dangerous bargaining position for activist women" since rather than arguing their position through intellect they were only able to "argue by numbers in the streets and be repelled by force".[^cite_note-Brooks-2008-210]

There are some contesting understandings in academic literature regarding the reasons behind the mobilization of women. While some argue that the micro-level actions of women can be understood through religious and political ideologies, others argue that it is in fact the effect of manipulations of information, symbols and context which should be studied.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENashat1983\[\[Category\:Wikipedia_articles_needing_page_number_citations_from_January_2022\]\]<sup_class="noprint_Inline-Template_"_style="white-space\:nowrap;">\[<i>\[\[Wikipedia\:Citing_sources|<span_title="This_citation_requires_a_reference_to_the_specific_page_or_range_of_pages_in_which_the_material_appears. (January_2022)">page needed</span>\]\]</i>\]</sup>-206]

## Aftermath

Main article: [Aftermath of the Iranian Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aftermath_of_the_Iranian_Revolution)

From early 1979 to either 1982 or 1983 [Iran](14653.md) was in a "revolutionary crisis mode."[^cite_note-Martín2004-217] After the system of [despotic monarchy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Despotic_monarchy) had been overthrown,[^cite_note-Moaddel2013-218] the economy and the apparatus of government had collapsed, and military and security forces were in disarray. Yet, by 1982 Khomeini and his supporters had crushed the rival factions, defeated local rebellions and consolidated power.

At the same time, events that made up both the crisis and its resolution were the [Iran hostage crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran_hostage_crisis), the [invasion of Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran%E2%80%93Iraq_War) by [Saddam Hussein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saddam_Hussein)'s Iraq, and the presidency of [Abolhassan Banisadr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abolhassan_Banisadr).[^cite_note-Martín2004-217][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKeddie2003241-219]

### Ayatollah Khomeini's consolidation of power

#### Conflicts among revolutionaries

Some observers believe "what began as an authentic and anti-dictatorial popular revolution based on a broad coalition of all anti-Shah forces was soon transformed into an [Islamic fundamentalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_fundamentalist) power-grab,"[^cite_note-220] and that except for his core supporters, the members of the coalition thought Khomeini intended to be more of a spiritual guide than a ruler.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESchirazi199793–94-221] Khomeini was in his mid-70s, never held public office, had been out of Iran for more than a decade, and told questioners "the religious dignitaries do not want to rule."[^cite_note-Matini-222] However, nobody could deny the unanimous central role of the [Imam](15240.md), and the other factions were too small to have any real impact.

Another view is Khomeini had "overwhelming ideological, political and organizational hegemony,"[^cite_note-223] and non-theocratic groups never seriously challenged Khomeini's movement in popular support.[^cite_note-225] Supporters of the new rule themselves have claimed that Iranians who opposed Khomeini were "[fifth columnists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fifth_column)" led by foreign countries attempting to overthrow the Iranian government.[^cite_note-Awakening-226]

Khomeini and his loyalists in the [revolutionary organizations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organizations_of_the_Iranian_Revolution) implemented Khomeini's [velayat-e faqih](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hokumat-e_Islami_:_Velayat-e_faqih_\(book_by_Khomeini\)) design for an Islamic republic led by himself as [supreme leader](36814344.md)[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESchirazi199724–32-227] by exploiting temporary allies[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMoin2000224-228] such as Mehdi Bazargan's [Provisional Government of Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Provisional_Government_of_Iran), whom they later eliminated from Iran's political stage one by one.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMoin2000203-229]
