The most important bodies of the revolution were the [Revolutionary Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_the_Islamic_Revolution), the [Revolutionary Guards](1285472.md), [Revolutionary Tribunals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_Revolutionary_Court), [Islamic Republican Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_Republican_Party), and [Revolutionary Committees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_Revolution_Committees) (*komitehs*).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKeddie2003241–242-230]

While the moderate Bazargan and his government (temporarily) reassured the middle class, it became apparent they did not have power over the "Khomeinist" revolutionary bodies, particularly the Revolutionary Council (the "real power" in the revolutionary state),[^cite_note-231][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEArjomand1988135-232] and later the Islamic Republican Party. Inevitably, the overlapping authority of the Revolutionary Council (which had the power to pass laws) and Bazargan's government was a source of conflict[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKeddie2003245-233] despite the fact that both had been approved by or put in place by Khomeini.

This conflict lasted only a few months however. The provisional government fell shortly after American Embassy officials were taken [hostage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran_hostage_crisis) on 4 November 1979. Bazargan's resignation was received by Khomeini without complaint, saying "Mr. Bazargan ... was a little tired and preferred to stay on the sidelines for a while." Khomeini later described his appointment of Bazargan as a "mistake."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMoin2000222-234]

The Revolutionary Guard, or *Pasdaran-e Enqelab*, was established by Khomeini on 5 May 1979, as a counterweight both to the armed groups of the left, and to the Shah's military. The guard eventually grew into "a full-scale" military force,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMackey1996371-235] becoming "the strongest institution of the revolution."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESchirazi1997151-236]

Serving under the Pasdaran are the *[Baseej-e Mostaz'afin](2892082.md)*, volunteers in everything from earthquake emergency management to attacking opposition demonstrators and newspaper offices.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKeddie2003275-237] The Islamic Republican Party[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMoin2000210–211-238] then fought to establish a theocratic government by *[velayat-e faqih](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Velayat-e_faqih)*.

Thousands of *komiteh* or [Revolutionary Committees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_Revolution_Committees)[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBakhash198456-239] served as "the eyes and ears" of the new rule and are credited by critics with "many arbitrary arrests, executions and confiscations of property."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMoin2000211-240]

Also enforcing the will of the government were the [Hezbollahi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hezbollahi) (the Party of God), "strong-arm thugs" who attacked demonstrators and offices of newspapers critical of Khomeini.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESchirazi1997153-241]

Two major political groups that formed after the fall of the Shah that clashed with and were eventually suppressed by pro-Khomeini groups, were the moderate religious [Muslim People's Republican Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_People's_Republican_Party) (MPRP) which was associated with Grand [Mohammad Kazem Shariatmadari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammad_Kazem_Shariatmadari), and the secular leftist [National Democratic Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Democratic_Front_\(Iran\)) (NDF).

#### 1979 ethnic uprisings

Further information: [1979 Khuzestan insurgency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1979_Khuzestan_insurgency) and [1979 Kurdish rebellion in Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1979_Kurdish_rebellion_in_Iran)

Following the events of the revolution, Marxist guerrillas and federalist parties revolted in some regions comprising [Khuzistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1979_Khuzestan_uprising), [Kurdistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1979_Kurdish_rebellion_in_Iran) and [Gonbad-e Qabus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gonbad-e_Qabus), which resulted in fighting between them and revolutionary forces. These revolts began in April 1979 and lasted between several months to over a year, depending on the region.

### Establishment of Islamic republic government

#### Referendum of 12 Farvardin

Main article: [1979 Iranian Islamic Republic referendum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1979_Iranian_Islamic_Republic_referendum)

On 30 and 31 March (Farvardin 10, 11) [a referendum was held](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1979_Iranian_Islamic_Republic_referendum) over whether to replace the monarchy with an "Islamic republic". Khomeini called for a massive turnout[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBakhash198473-242] and only the [National Democratic Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Democratic_Front_of_Iran), [Fadayan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organization_of_Iranian_People's_Fedaian_\(Majority\)), and several Kurdish parties opposed the vote.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBakhash198473-242] The results showed that 98.2% had voted in favor of the Islamic Republic.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBakhash198473-242]

#### Writing of the constitution

Main articles: [Assembly for the Final Review of the Constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assembly_for_the_Final_Review_of_the_Constitution), [1979 Iranian Constitutional Assembly election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1979_Iranian_Constitutional_Assembly_election), [1979 Iranian constitutional referendum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1979_Iranian_constitutional_referendum), and [Interim Government of Iran (1979–80)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interim_Government_of_Iran_\(1979%E2%80%9380\))

In June 1979 the [Freedom Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_Movement_of_Iran) released its draft constitution for the Islamic Republic that it had been working on since Khomeini was in exile. It included a [Guardian Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guardian_Council) to veto un-Islamic legislation, but had no guardian jurist ruler.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMoin2000217-243] Leftists found the draft too conservative and in need of major changes but Khomeini declared it 'correct'.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESchirazi199722–23-244] To approve the new constitution and prevent leftist alterations, a relatively small seventy-three-member [Assembly of Experts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1st_Assembly_of_Experts) for Constitution was elected that summer. Critics complained that "vote-rigging, violence against undesirable candidates and the dissemination of false information" was used to "produce an assembly overwhelmingly dominated by clergy, all took active roles during the revolution and loyal to Khomeini."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMoin2000218-245]

Khomeini (and the assembly) now rejected the constitution – its correctness notwithstanding – and Khomeini declared that the new government should be based "100% on Islam."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBakhash198474–82-246]

In addition to the president, the new constitution included a more powerful post of guardian jurist ruler intended for Khomeini,[^cite_note-iranonline-247] with control of the military and security services, and power to appoint several top government and judicial officials. It increased the power and number of clerics on the [Council of Guardians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_Guardians) and gave it control over elections[^cite_note-248] as well as laws passed by the legislature.

The new constitution was also approved overwhelmingly by the December 1979 constitutional referendum, but with more opposition[^cite_note-249] and smaller turnout.[^cite_note-250]

### Hostage crisis

Main article: [Iran hostage crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran_hostage_crisis)

In late October 1979, the exiled and dying Shah was admitted into the United States for cancer treatment. In Iran there was an immediate outcry, and both Khomeini and leftist groups demanded the Shah's return to Iran for trial and execution. On 4 November 1979 youthful Islamists, calling themselves [Muslim Student Followers of the Imam's Line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_Student_Followers_of_the_Imam's_Line), invaded the US embassy compound in Tehran and [seized its staff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran_hostage_crisis). Revolutionaries were angry because of how the Shah had left Iran which spawned rumors of another US–backed coup in Iran that would re-install him. The occupation was also intended as leverage to demand the return of the Shah to stand trial in exchange for the hostages, and depose Prime Minister Mehdi Bazargan, who they believed was plotting to normalize relations with the US. The students held 52 American diplomats hostage for 444 days, which played a role in helping to pass the constitution, suppressing moderates, and otherwise radicalising the revolution.[^cite_note-251]

Holding the hostages was very popular and continued even after the death of the Shah. As Khomeini explained to his future President [Banisadr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banisadr), "This action has many benefits. ...This has united our people. Our opponents do not dare act against us. We can put the constitution to the people's vote without difficulty..."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMoin2000228-252]

With great publicity the students released documents from the American embassy, which they labeled a "den of spies",[^cite_note-253] showing that moderate Iranian leaders had met with US officials (and did not release similar evidence of high-ranking Islamists having done the same).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMoin2000248–249-254] Among the casualties of the hostage crisis was Prime Minister Bazargan and his government, who resigned in November unable to enforce the government's order to release the hostages.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKeddie2003249-255]

The prestige of Khomeini and the hostage taking was further enhanced with the failure of a hostage rescue attempt, widely credited to divine intervention.[^cite_note-256]

The hostage crisis ended with the signing of the [Algiers Accords](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Algiers_Accords_\(1981\)) in Algeria on 19 January 1981. The hostages were formally released to United States custody the following day, just minutes after [Ronald Reagan](25433.md) was sworn in as the new American president.

### Suppression of opposition

See also: [Nojeh coup plot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nojeh_coup_plot)



A revolutionary firing squad in 27 August 1979

Khomeini and his followers claimed sole credit for the revolution, sidelining other revolutionary groups. This was possible due to the regime's control over public opinion and historical narrative.[^cite_note-257] In early March 1979, Khomeini announced, "do not use this term, 'democratic.' That is the Western style," giving pro-democracy liberals (and later leftists) a taste of disappointments to come.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBakhash198473-242] In succession the [National Democratic Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Democratic_Front_\(Iran\)) was banned in August 1979, the provisional government was disempowered in November, the [Muslim People's Republican Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_People's_Republican_Party) was banned in January 1980, the [People's Mujahedin of Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's_Mujahedin_of_Iran) guerrillas came under attack in February 1980, a purge of universities started in March 1980, and the liberal Islamist President [Abolhassan Banisadr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abolhassan_Banisadr) was [impeached](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Impeached) in June 1981.[^cite_note-pay13-258]



Executed generals of the Imperial Iranian Army: [Reza Naji](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reza_Naji_\(Iranian_general\)), [Mehdi Rahimi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mehdi_Rahimi), and [Manouchehr Khosrodad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manouchehr_Khosrodad)

After the revolution, human rights groups estimated the number of casualties suffered by protesters and prisoners of the new system to be several thousand. The first to be executed were members of the old system – senior generals, followed by over 200 senior civilian officials[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMoin2000208-259] – as punishment and to eliminate the danger of a coup d'état. Brief trials lacking defense attorneys, juries, transparency or the opportunity for the accused to defend themselves[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBakhash198461-260] were held by revolutionary judges such as [Sadegh Khalkhali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sadegh_Khalkhali), the *[Sharia](28840.md)* judge. By January 1980 "at least 582 persons had been executed."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMackey1996291-261] Among those executed was [Amir Abbas Hoveida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amir_Abbas_Hoveida), former Prime Minister of Iran.[^cite_note-262] Between January 1980 and June 1981, when Bani-Sadr was impeached, at least 900 executions took place,[^cite_note-263] for everything from drug and sexual offenses to "corruption on earth," from plotting counter-revolution and spying for Israel to membership in opposition groups.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBakhash1984111-264]

#### The 1981–1982 massacre

See also: [1981–1982 Iran Massacres](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1981%E2%80%931982_Iran_Massacres)

Between June 1981 and March 1982, the theocratic regime carried out the largest political massacre in Iranian history, targeting communists, socialists, social democrats, liberals, monarchists, moderate Islamists, and members of the Baha'i faith as part of the Iranian Cultural Revolution decreed by Khomeini on 14 June 1980 with the intent of "purifying" Iranian society of non-Islamic elements.[^cite_note-Nasiri-2022-265] Between June 1981 and June 1982, Amnesty International documented 2,946 executions, with several thousand more killed in the next two years according to the anti-government guerilla [People's Mujahedin of Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's_Mujahedin_of_Iran).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBakhash1984221–222-266] More recently, Rastyad Collective has verified the identities of more than 3,400 political dissidents who were executed between June 1981 and March 1982.[^cite_note-Rastyad-267][^cite_note-268] These dissidents were sentenced to death by the Islamic Revolutionary Courts during show trials in more than eighty-five cities across the country on charges of spreading "corruption on Earth" (*ifsad-fi-alarz*), "espionage", "terrorism", or "enmity against Allah" (*Moharebeh*).[^cite_note-Nasiri-2022-265] Most victims of the 1981 massacre were young activists aged eleven to twenty-four. These activists were either high school students or had recently graduated from universities in Iran and abroad. During the massacre, hundreds of minors were also subjected to arbitrary detention, torture, and summary executions on ideologically motivated charges of *ifsad-fi-alarz* and *moharebeh* by the revolutionary courts.[^cite_note-Rastyad-267][^cite_note-Nasiri-2022-265][^cite_note-269] In July 2024, The Special Rapporteur published a landmark UN Report on the 1981 massacre and categorised the atrocity crimes committed in 1981 and 1982 as genocide and crimes against humanity. In this report, the Rapporteur called for the establishment of an independent and international accountability mechanism.[^cite_note-270]

#### Closing of non-Islamist newspapers

In mid-August 1979, shortly after the election of the constitution-writing assembly, several dozen newspapers and magazines opposing Khomeini's idea of theocratic rule by jurists were shut down.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESchirazi199751-271][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMoin2000219–220-272][^cite_note-273] When protests were organized by the [National Democratic Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Democratic_Front_\(Iran\)) (NDF), Khomeini angrily denounced them saying, "we thought we were dealing with human beings. It is evident we are not."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMoin2000219-274]

> ... After each revolution several thousand of these corrupt elements are executed in public and burnt and the story is over. They are not allowed to publish newspapers.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMoin2000219-274]

Hundreds were injured by "rocks, clubs, chains and iron bars" when [Hezbollahi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hezbollahi) attacked the protesters,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMoin2000219–220-272] and shortly after, a warrant was issued for the arrest of the NDF's leader.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBakhash198489-275]

#### Muslim People's Republican Party



[Kazem Shariatmadari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kazem_Shariatmadari) and Ayatollah Khomeini

In December the moderate Islamic party [Muslim People's Republican Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_People's_Republican_Party) (MPRP) and its spiritual leader [Mohammad Kazem Shariatmadari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammad_Kazem_Shariatmadari) had become a rallying point for Iranians who wanted democracy not theocracy.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMoin2000232-276] Riots broke out in Shariatmadari's Azeri home region with members of the MPRP and Shariatmadari's followers seizing the [Tabriz](439791.md) television station and using it to "broadcast demands and grievances." The regime reacted quickly, sending Revolutionary Guards to retake the TV station, mediators to defuse complaints and activists to stage a massive pro-Khomeini counter-demonstration.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBakhash198489–90-277] The party was suppressed,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMoin2000232-276] and in 1982 Shariatmadari was "demoted" from the rank of Grand Ayatollah and many of his clerical followers were purged.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEArjomand1988156-278]

#### Islamist left

Main article: [Socialism in Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialism_in_Iran)

In January 1980, [Abolhassan Banisadr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abolhassan_Banisadr) was elected president of Iran. Though an adviser to Khomeini, he was a leftist who clashed with another ally of Khomeini, the theocratic [Islamic Republic Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_Republic_Party) (IRP) – the controlling power in the new parliament.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMoin2000234–235-279]

At the same time, erstwhile revolutionary allies of Khomeini – the Islamist modernist guerrilla group [People's Mujahedin of Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's_Mujahedin_of_Iran) (the MEK) – were being suppressed by Khomeini's forces. Khomeini attacked the MEK, referring to them as *[monafeqin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Munafiq)&#x20;*(hypocrites) and *[kafer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kafir)* (unbelievers).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMoin2000234,_239-280] *Hezbollahi* people attacked meeting places, bookstores, and newsstands of Mujahideen and other leftists,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBakhash1984123-281] driving them underground. Universities were closed to purge them of opponents of theocratic rule as a part of the "[Cultural Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_Cultural_Revolution)", and 20,000 teachers and nearly 8,000 military officers deemed too westernized were dismissed.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEArjomand1988144-282]

By mid-1981 matters came to a head. An attempt by Khomeini to forge a reconciliation between Banisadr and IRP leaders had failed,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBakhash1984153-283] and now it was Banisadr who was the rallying point "for all doubters and dissidents" of the theocracy, including the MEK.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMoin2000238-284]

When leaders of the [National Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Front_\(Iran\)) called for a demonstration in June 1981 in favor of Banisadr, Khomeini threatened its leaders with the death penalty for [apostasy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apostasy_in_Islam) "if they did not repent".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESchirazi1997127-285] Leaders of the [Freedom Movement of Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_Movement_of_Iran) were compelled to make and publicly broadcast apologies for supporting the Front's appeal.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESchirazi1997127-285] Those attending the rally were menaced by Hezbollahi and Revolutionary Guards and intimidated into silence.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBakhash1984158–159-286]

On 28 June 1981, a bombing of the office of the IRP killed around 70 high-ranking officials, cabinet members and members of parliament, including [Mohammad Beheshti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammad_Beheshti), the secretary-general of the party and head of the Islamic Republic's judicial system. The government arrested thousands, and there were hundreds of executions against the MEK and its followers.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMoin2000241–242-287] Despite these and other assassinations[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMoin2000210–211-238] the hoped-for mass uprising and armed struggle against the Khomeiniists was crushed.

In May 1979, the [Furqan Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Furqan_Group) (*Guruh-i Furqan*) assassinated an important lieutenant of Khomeini, [Morteza Motahhari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Morteza_Motahhari).[^cite_note-288]

## Impacts

### International impact

Further information: [1979 oil crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1979_oil_crisis)

Internationally, the initial impact of the revolution was immense. In the non-Muslim world, it changed the image of Islam, generating much interest in Islam—both sympathetic[^cite_note-289] and hostile[^cite_note-nasr-290]—and even speculation that the revolution might change "the world balance of power more than any political event since Hitler's conquest of Europe."[^cite_note-291]

The Islamic Republic positioned itself as a revolutionary beacon under the slogan "neither East nor West, only Islamic Republic" ("Na Sharq, Na Gharb, Faqat Jumhuri-e Islami," i.e., neither Soviet nor American / West European models) and called for the overthrow of [capitalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capitalism), American influence, and social injustice in the Middle East and the rest of the world. Revolutionary leaders in Iran gave and sought support from non-Muslim activists such as the [Sandinistas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sandinistas) in Nicaragua, the [Irish Republican Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Provisional_Irish_Republican_Army) (IRA) in Ireland and the anti-[apartheid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apartheid) struggle in South Africa, even favoring far-left revolutionaries over Islamist (but ideologically different and strategically harmful) causes such as the neighboring [Afghan Mujahideen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet%E2%80%93Afghan_War).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERoy1994175-292] In terms of future relevance, the conflicts that originated from the Iranian Revolution continued to define geo-politics for the following decades.[^cite_note-293]

The revolution itself was supported by the [Palestine Liberation Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestine_Liberation_Organization) (PLO).[^cite_note-294] [Chairman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chairman_of_the_Palestine_Liberation_Organization) [Yasser Arafat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yasser_Arafat) visited Tehran in February 1979 and received a commitment from Ayatollah Khomeini that Iran would promptly "turn to the issue of victory over Israel," while Arafat cited how the revolution in Iran had "turned upside down" the area's strategic balance, including through the PLO's support of underground fighters in Iran.[^cite_note-295]

In February 1981, the Islamic Republic supported [Hunger strikes in the H-Block of the HM Prison Maze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1981_Irish_hunger_strike) by Irish Republicans including [Bobby Sands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bobby_Sands). Following the death of Sands in May 1981, the street in Tehran the British Embassy is on was renamed from "Winston Churchill Street" to "Bobby Sands Street".[^cite_note-Bonakdarian-2018-296] The IRA saw the Iranian Revolution as aspirational, an example of mass action as a way to meet goals. Both the Iranian government and their opposition supported the hunger strikes, creating a competition for the favor of the IRA. The support by the government came after the UK sided with Iraq and Saddam Hussein in the Iran–Iraq war. Oppositional groups like [Mojahedin-e-Khalq](285632.md) claimed solidarity with the hunger strike and connected their Marxist influences. The Iranian government, Iranian leftist groups, and the IRA saw Britain as a colonizing force in both regions. For a time, Mojahedin-e-Khalq and the [Provisional Sinn Féin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Provisional_Sinn_F%C3%A9in) (the political arm of the [Provisional IRA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Provisional_IRA)) had consistent contact.[^cite_note-Bonakdarian-2018-296] Contact ended in June 1981 after fear of offending the Iranian Government.[^cite_note-Bonakdarian-2018-296]

The Islamic Republic changed the foreign policy of the Pahlavi dynasty by supporting [third worldist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third-worldism) movements like the anti-apartheid movement in South Africa. The Pahlavis and South Africa had relations dating back to when Reza Shah went there in exile. After the first Shah's ousting by the Soviets and British during World War II, he spent the remainder of his life in Johannesburg.[^cite_note-297] Pre-revolution Iran provided 90% of South Africa's oil.[^cite_note-Chehabi-2016-298] After the revolution, Iran cut South Africa off from its oil supply and financially supported the African National Congress.[^cite_note-Chehabi-2016-298] This was part of the larger want by Khomeini to support the "downtrodden" of the world, including, non-aligned countries. In 1980, the ANC attended the "Gather of World Liberation" hosted in Tehran for the third worldist movement.[^cite_note-299] In 2015, the International Relations and Cooperation minister of South Africa said of Iran, "The Islamic Republic stood by us during our darkest days, cutting ties with the apartheid regime. Your revolution was our revolution. You showed us that emancipation was possible, whatever the odds."[^cite_note-300] A connection with the ANC continued even when tested during the apartheid era and the Iran–Iraq war. In 1985, Iran and South Africa traded weapons for oil.[^cite_note-301] The belief in third-worldism seemed to have superseded the change in behavior by Iran.

### Persian Gulf and the Iran–Iraq War

Main article: [Iran–Iraq War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran%E2%80%93Iraq_War)



Obverse



Reverse

Iranian 20 Rials coin – monument of 3rd anniversary of Iranian Revolution

Supporters of the revolution both within and outside of Iran began calling for the overthrow of monarchies in the region and for them to be replaced by Islamic republics. This alarmed many of Iran's neighbours, particularly Kuwait, Iraq and Saudi Arabia as well as Western nations dependent on Middle Eastern oil for their energy needs.

In September 1980, Iraq took advantage of the febrile situation and [invaded Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraqi_invasion_of_Iran). At the centre of Iraq's objectives was the annexation of the East Bank of the Shaat Al-Arab waterway that makes up part of the border between the two nations and which had been the site of numerous border skirmishes between the two countries going back to the late 1960s. The president of Iraq, [Saddam Hussein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saddam_Hussein), also wanted to annex the Iranian province of [Khuzestan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khuzestan), substantially populated by Iranian Arabs. There was also concern that a Shia-centric revolution in Iran may stimulate a similar uprising in Iraq, where the country's Sunni minority ruled over the Shia majority.

Hussein was confident that with Iraq's armed forces being well-equipped with new technology and with high morale would enjoy a decisive strategic advantage against an Iranian military that had recently had much of its command officers purged following the revolution. Iran was also struggling to find replacement parts for much of its US- and British-supplied equipment. Hussein believed that victory would therefore come swiftly.

However, Iran was "galvanized"[^cite_note-302] by the invasion and the populace of Iran rallied behind their new government in an effort to repel the invaders. After some early successes, the Iraqi invasion stalled and was then repelled and by 1982, Iran had recaptured almost all of its territories. In June 1982, with Iraqi forces all but expelled from Iranian territory, the Iraqi government offered a ceasefire. This was rejected by Khomeini, who declared that the only condition for peace was that "the regime in [Baghdad](4492.md) must fall and must be replaced by an Islamic republic".[^cite_note-303]

The war would continue for another six years during which time countries like Saudi Arabia, Kuwait and other Gulf states provided financial assistance to Iraq in an effort to prevent an Iranian victory, even though their relations with Iraq were often hostile – Kuwait itself was [invaded by Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraqi_invasion_of_Kuwait) two years after the peace agreement between Iraq and Iran was signed.

The war served in part as an opportunity for the government to strengthen revolutionary ardour and revolutionary groups; the [Revolutionary Guard](1285472.md) and committees at the expense of its remaining allies-turned-opponents, such as the MEK.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKeddie2003241,_251-304][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBakhash1984128–129-305] While enormously costly and destructive, the war "rejuvenate\[d] the drive for national unity and Islamic revolution" and "inhibited fractious debate and dispute" in Iran.[^cite_note-306]

### Foreign relations

Main articles: [Iran–United Kingdom relations](4294366.md), [Iran–Israel relations](3022965.md), [Iran–Russia relations](3912038.md), and [China–Iran relations](29416684.md)

See also: [Foreign relations of Iran](64709.md) and [Iran–Israel proxy conflict](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran%E2%80%93Israel_proxy_conflict)

The Islamic Republic of Iran experienced difficult relations with some Western countries, especially the United States, Saudi Arabia, and the Eastern Bloc nations led by the Soviet Union. Iran was under constant [US unilateral sanctions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanctions_against_Iran), which were tightened under the presidency of [Bill Clinton](3356.md). Most European countries, despite their significant investments within Iran, stood in solidarity with the United States and also imposed economic sanctions on the theocratic regime.[^cite_note-307] Britain suspended all diplomatic relations with Iran and did not re-open their embassy in Tehran until 1988.[^cite_note-Neville-308] The European country that maintained the closest relations with the new Iranian government was Switzerland, which was neither a part of the [European Economic Community](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Economic_Community) nor the [North Atlantic Treaty Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Atlantic_Treaty_Organization).[^cite_note-309] Relations with the USSR became strained as well after the Soviet government condemned Khomeini's repression of certain minorities after the revolution.[^cite_note-310] Diplomatic relations between Iran and the [apartheid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apartheid) government of South Africa were completely severed following Khomeini's rise to power.[^cite_note-311]

In 1950, Iran became the second Muslim-majority country to extend de facto recognition to Israel following its establishment in 1948.[^cite_note-312] After the Shah regained his throne after the [1953 Iranian coup d'état](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1953_Iranian_coup_d'%C3%A9tat) that overthrew prime minister [Mohammad Mossadegh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammad_Mossadegh), Iran expanded relations between the two nations, posting ambassadors to each other, establishing military contacts and supplying more than half of Israel's oil.[^cite_note-313][^cite_note-314] Relations were cut on 18 February 1979 when Iran adopted an [anti-Zionist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Zionist) stance, with the former [Embassy of Israel, Tehran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Embassy_of_Israel,_Tehran) handed over to the [Palestine Liberation Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestine_Liberation_Organization).[^cite_note-315] Iran has allied and funded several [anti-Israeli Islamist militant groups](36658251.md) since.[^cite_note-316]

After the US sanctions were tightened and the collapse of the Soviet Union, the Russian Federation and the People's Republic of China became the principal allies for Iran.[^cite_note-317] Relations between the two countries improved [after Vladimir Putin took office in 2000](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russia_under_Vladimir_Putin) and increasingly warmer in recent years following an international backlash over the annexation of Crimea in 2014 which led to sanctions by the Western powers. Russia had sought Iran on expanding arms trade over the past three decades especially with the cooperation with the Assad government during the [Syrian civil war](30741795.md).[^cite_note-318][^cite_note-319] Iran also began its [economic cooperation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran%E2%80%93China_25-year_Cooperation_Program) with China that includes "political, strategic and economic" components between the two nations.[^cite_note-320][^cite_note-321][^cite_note-322][^cite_note-323]

### In the Muslim world

See also: [The policy of exporting the Islamic Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_policy_of_exporting_the_Islamic_Revolution) and [Iran–Saudi Arabia proxy conflict](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran%E2%80%93Saudi_Arabia_proxy_conflict)

In the Muslim world, particularly in its early years, the revolution inspired enormous enthusiasm and redoubled opposition to western imperialism, intervention and influence. Islamist insurgents rose in [Saudi Arabia (1979)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Mosque_Seizure), [Egypt (1981)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anwar_Al_Sadat#Assassination), [Syria (1982)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1982_Hama_massacre), and [Lebanon (1983)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/April_1983_United_States_Embassy_bombing).[^cite_note-Kramer-324]

In [Pakistan](23235.md), it has been noted that the "press was largely favorable towards the new government"; the [Islamist](15012.md) parties were even more enthusiastic; while the ruler, General [Zia-ul-Haq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zia-ul-Haq), himself on an [Islamization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad_Zia-ul-Haq's_Islamization) drive since he took power in 1977, talked of "simultaneous triumph of Islamic ideology in both our countries" and that "Khomeini is a symbol of Islamic insurgence." Some American analysts noted that, at this point, Khomeini's influence and prestige in Pakistan was greater than Zia-ul-Haq's himself.[^cite_note-325] After Khomeini claimed that Americans were behind the 1979 [Grand Mosque seizure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Mosque_seizure), student protesters from the [Quaid-e-Azam University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quaid-e-Azam_University) in [Islamabad](51153.md) [attacked the US embassy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1979_U.S._embassy_burning_in_Islamabad), setting it on fire and taking hostages. While the crisis was quickly defused by the Pakistan military, the next day, before some 120 Pakistani army officers stationed in Iran on the road to [hajj](23487999.md), Khomeini said, "it is a cause of joy that ... all Pakistan has risen against the United States" and the struggle is not that of the US and Iran but "the entire world of disbelief and the world of Islam." According to journalist [Yaroslav Trofimov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yaroslav_Trofimov), "the Pakistani officers, many of whom had graduated from Western military academies, seemed swayed by the ayatollah's intoxicating words."[^cite_note-326]

Ultimately only the Lebanese Islamists succeeded. The Islamic revolutionary government itself is credited with helping establish [Hezbollah](13919.md) in Lebanon[^cite_note-327] and the [Supreme Council for the Islamic Revolution in Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Council_for_the_Islamic_Revolution_in_Iraq).

On the other side of the ledger, at least one observer argues that despite great effort and expense the only countries outside Iran the revolution had a "measure of lasting influence" on are Lebanon and Iraq.[^cite_note-328] Others claim the devastating [Iran–Iraq War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran%E2%80%93Iraq_War) "mortally wounded ... the ideal of spreading the Islamic revolution,"[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKeddie2003241-219] or that the Islamic Republic's pursuit of an ideological rather than a "nationalist, pragmatic" foreign policy has weakened Iran's "place as a great regional power".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERoy1994193-329]

### Domestic impact

Further information: [History of the Islamic Republic of Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Islamic_Republic_of_Iran) and [Interim Government of Iran (1981)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interim_Government_of_Iran_\(1981\))

Views differ on the impact of the revolution.[^cite_note-331] For some it was "the most significant, hopeful and profound event in the entirety of contemporary Islamic history",[^cite_note-332] while other Iranians believe that the revolution was a time when "for a few years we all lost our minds",[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShirley199798,_104,_195-333] and which "promised us heaven, but... created a hell on earth."[^cite_note-334]



People celebrating anniversary of the revolution in [Mashhad](406512.md) in 2014

Internally, Iran has had some success with the [governmental promotion of Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cultural_Revolution_in_Iran), and the elimination of [secularism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secularism) and [American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_the_United_States) [influence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Americanization_\(foreign_culture_and_media\)) in government. Criticisms have been raised with regards to [political freedom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_freedom), governmental [honesty](66220.md) and [efficiency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Good_governance), [economic equality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_egalitarianism) and [self-sufficiency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Self-sufficiency), or even popular religious devotion.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERoy1994199-335][^cite_note-336] Opinion polls and observers report widespread dissatisfaction, including a "rift" between the revolutionary generation and younger Iranians who find it "impossible to understand what their parents were so passionate about."[^cite_note-337] To honor the 40th anniversary of revolution around 50,000 prisoners were forgiven by order [Ali Khamenei](385653.md) to receive "Islamic [clemency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clemency)".[^cite_note-338][^cite_note-339][^cite_note-340] Many religious minorities such as Christians, [Baháʼís](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bah%C3%A1%CA%BC%C3%ADs), [Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exodus_of_Iranian_Jews) and [Zoroastrians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zoroastrians) have had to flee Iran since the Islamic Revolution of 1979.[^cite_note-341][^cite_note-342]

Literacy has continued to increase under the Islamic Republic.[^cite_note-343][^cite_note-344] By 2002, illiteracy rates dropped by more than half.[^cite_note-345][^cite_note-UNGEI-346] Maternal and infant mortality rates have also been cut significantly.[^cite_note-347] Population growth was first encouraged but discouraged after 1988.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKeddie2003287–288-348] Overall, Iran's Human development Index rating has climbed significantly from 0.569 in 1980 to 0.732 in 2002, on a par with neighbouring Turkey.[^cite_note-349][^cite_note-350] In the latest HDI, however, Iran has since fallen 8 ranks below Turkey.[^cite_note-351]

### Politics and government

Main article: [Politics of Iran](64702.md)

Iran has elected governmental bodies at the national, provincial, and local levels. Although these bodies are subordinate to theocracy – which has veto power over who can run for parliament (or [Islamic Consultative Assembly](383276.md)) and whether its bills can become law – they have more power than equivalent organs in the Shah's government.

Iran's Sunni minority (about 8%) has seen some unrest.[^cite_note-352] Five of the 290 parliamentary seats are allocated to their communities.[^cite_note-353]

The members of the [Baháʼí Faith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bah%C3%A1%CA%BC%C3%AD_Faith) have been declared [heretical and subversive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persecution_of_Bah%C3%A1%CA%BC%C3%ADs).[^cite_note-354] While persecution occurred before the revolution since then more than 200 Baháʼís have been executed or presumed killed, and many more have been imprisoned, deprived of jobs, pensions, businesses, and educational opportunities. Baháʼí holy places have been confiscated, vandalized, or destroyed. More recently, Baháʼís in Iran have been deprived of education and work. Several thousand young Baháʼís between the ages of 17 and 24 have been expelled from universities.

Whether the Islamic Republic has brought more or less severe political repression is disputed. Grumbling once done about the tyranny and corruption of the Shah and his court is now directed against "the Mullahs."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShirley1997\[\[Category\:Wikipedia_articles_needing_page_number_citations_from_January_2022\]\]<sup_class="noprint_Inline-Template_"_style="white-space\:nowrap;">\[<i>\[\[Wikipedia\:Citing_sources|<span_title="This_citation_requires_a_reference_to_the_specific_page_or_range_of_pages_in_which_the_material_appears. (January_2022)">page needed</span>\]\]</i>\]</sup>-355] Fear of SAVAK has been replaced by fear of [Revolutionary Guards](1285472.md), and other religious revolutionary enforcers.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESchirazi1997153-241] Violations of human rights by the theocratic government is said to be worse than during the monarchy,[^cite_note-356] and in any case extremely grave.[^cite_note-hrw\.o-357] Reports of [torture](47702.md), imprisonment of dissidents, and the murder of prominent critics have been made by human rights groups. [Censorship](4297092.md) is handled by the [Ministry of Culture and Islamic Guidance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Culture_and_Islamic_Guidance), without whose official permission, "no books or magazines are published, no audiotapes are distributed, no movies are shown and no cultural organization is established. Men and women are not allowed to dance or swim with each other."[^cite_note-358]

### Women

See also: [Women's rights in Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Women's_rights_in_Iran), [Women's rights movement in Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Women's_rights_movement_in_Iran), and [1979 International Women's Day protests in Tehran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1979_International_Women's_Day_protests_in_Tehran)

Throughout the beginning of the 20th century and prior to the revolution, many women leaders emerged and demanded basic social rights for women.[^cite_note-Mahnaz-359] During the reign of [Reza Shah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reza_Shah), the government [mandated the removal of the veil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kashf-e_hijab) and promoted the education of young girls.[^cite_note-Mahnaz-359] However, the push-back of the [Shia clerics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shia_clerics) made progress difficult, and the government had to contain its promotion of basic women's rights to the norms of the patriarchal social hierarchy to accommodate the clerics.[^cite_note-Mahnaz-359] After the abdication of [Reza Shah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reza_Shah) in 1941, the discipline of the government decreased, and women were able to further exercise their rights, including the ability to wear the veil if they wanted.[^cite_note-Mahnaz-359] More organization of women's groups occurred in the 1960s and 70s, and they used the government's modernization to define and advocate for women's issues.[^cite_note-Mahnaz-359] During these decades, women became active in formerly male domains such as the parliament, the cabinet, armed forces, legal professions, and fields of science and technology.[^cite_note-Mahnaz-359] Additionally, women achieved the right to vote in 1963.[^cite_note-Mahnaz-359] Many of these achievements and rights that Iranian women had gained in the decades leading up to the revolution were reversed by the Islamic Revolution.[^cite_note-Mahnaz-359]

The revolutionary government rewrote laws in an attempt to force women to leave the workforce by promoting the early retirement of female government employees, the closing of childcare centers, enforcing full Islamic cover in offices and public places, as well as preventing women from studying in 140 fields in higher education.[^cite_note-Mahnaz-359] Women [fought back](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1979_International_Women's_Day_protests_in_Tehran) against these changes, and as activist and writer [Mahnaz Afkhami](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahnaz_Afkhami) writes, "The regime succeeded in putting women back in the veil in public places, but not in resocializing them into fundamentalist norms."[^cite_note-Mahnaz-359] After the revolution, women often had to work hard to support their families as the post-revolutionary economy suffered.[^cite_note-Mahnaz-359] Women also asserted themselves in the arts, literature, education, and politics.[^cite_note-Mahnaz-359]

Women – especially those from traditional backgrounds – participated on a large scale in demonstrations leading up to the revolution.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGraham1980227-360] They were encouraged by [Ayatollah Khomeini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayatollah_Khomeini) to join him in overthrowing the [Pahlavi dynasty](338883.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEIsfandiyārī1997-212] However, most of these women expected the revolution to lead to an increase in their rights and opportunities rather than the restrictions that actually occurred.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEIsfandiyārī1997-212] The policy enacted by the revolutionary government and its attempts to limit the rights of women were challenged by the mobilization and politicization of women that occurred during and after the revolution.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEIsfandiyārī1997-212] Women's resistance included remaining in the work force in large numbers and challenging Islamic dress by showing hair under their head scarves.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEIsfandiyārī1997-212] The Iranian government has had to reconsider and change aspects of its policies towards women because of their resistance to laws that restrict their rights.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEIsfandiyārī1997-212]

Since the revolution, university enrollment and the number of women in the civil service and higher education has risen.[^cite_note-362] and several women have been elected to the [Iranian parliament](383276.md).

### Homosexuality

See also: [LGBTQ history in Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBTQ_history_in_Iran) and [LGBTQ rights in Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBTQ_rights_in_Iran)

When Ruhollah Khomeini came to power in 1979, he called for homosexuals to be "exterminated",[^cite_note-363] and one of his first political actions was to institute [imprisonment](213806.md), [corporal punishment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corporal_punishment), and the death penalty for any sexual acts outside traditional Islamic heterosexual marriage. In a 1979 interview with [The New York Times](30680.md), a journalist asked Khomeini to justify the state-sanctioned shootings of homosexuals. In reply Khomeini compared them as well as other adulterers to gangrene, thieves, and murderers.[^cite_note-364]

Iran is currently one of the world's only jurisdictions to actively execute gay men.[^cite_note-365][^cite_note-ILGA_2015-366][^cite_note-367] [Amnesty International](18947898.md) reports that approximately 5,000 homosexuals have been executed in Iran since the revolution.[^cite_note-368]

### Economic impact

See also: [Economy of Iran](64704.md) and [United States sanctions against Iran](9941859.md)

[Iran's post-revolutionary economy](64704.md) has a significant state-owned or [parastatal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parastatal) sector, including businesses owned by the [Revolutionary Guards](1285472.md) and [Bonyad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bonyad) foundations.[^cite_note-econ-7-3-15-369][^cite_note-yeg-11-13-370]

Since the revolution Iran's GDP (PPP) has grown from [\$](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geary%E2%80%93Khamis_dollar)114 billion in 1980 to \$858 billion in 2010.[^cite_note-IMFIran2010-371] GDP per capita (PPP) has grown from [\$](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geary%E2%80%93Khamis_dollar)4,295 in 1980 to \$11,396 in 2010.[^cite_note-IMFIran2010-371]

Since the revolution Iran's GDP (Nominal) has grown from \$90.392 billion in 1979 to \$385.874 in 2015.[^cite_note-372] GDP per capita (nominal) has grown from \$2290 in 1979 to \$5470 in 2016.[^cite_note-373] Real GNI per capita in 2011 constant international dollars decreased after the revolution and during the Iran-Iraq war from \$7762 in 1979 to \$3699 at the end of the war in 1989. After three decades of reconstruction and growth since then, it has not yet reached its 1979 level and has only recovered to \$6751 in 2016.[^cite_note-374] Data on GNI per capita in PPP terms is only available since 1990 globally. In PPP terms, GNI per capita has increased from Int. \$11,425 in 1990 to Int. \$18,544 in 2016. But most of this increase can be attributed to the rise in oil prices in the 2000s.[^cite_note-375]

The value of Iran's currency declined precipitously after the revolution. Whereas on 15 March 1978, 71.46 rials equaled one US dollar, in January 2018, 44,650 rials amounted to one dollar.[^cite_note-376]

The economy has become slightly more diversified since the revolution, with 80% of Iranian GDP dependent on oil and gas as of 2010.[^cite_note-377] The Islamic Republic lags some countries in transparency and ease of doing business according to international surveys. [Transparency International](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transparency_International) ranked Iran 136th out of 175 countries in transparency (i.e., lack of corruption) for its 2014 index;[^cite_note-econ-7-3-15-369] and the IRI was ranked 130th out of the 189 countries surveyed in the [World Bank](36752.md) 2015 Doing Business Report.[^cite_note-DB2015-378]

### Islamic political culture

It is said that there were attempts to incorporate modern political and social concepts into Islamic canon since 1950. The attempt was a reaction to the secular political discourse, namely [Marxism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marxism), liberalism and nationalism. Following the death of Ayatollah Boroujerdi, some of the scholars like [Murtaza Mutahhari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Murtaza_Mutahhari), [Muhammad Beheshti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad_Beheshti) and [Mahmoud Taleghani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahmoud_Taleghani) found new opportunity to change conditions. Before them, Boroujerdi was considered a conservative Marja. They tried to reform conditions after the death of the ayatollah. They presented their arguments by rendering lectures in 1960 and 1963 in Tehran. The result of the lectures was the book *[An Inquiry into Principles of Mar'jaiyat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/An_Inquiry_into_Principles_of_Mar'jaiyat?action=edit\&redlink=1)*. Some of the major issues highlighted were the government in Islam, the need for the clergy's independent financial organization, Islam as a way of life, advising and guiding youth and necessity of being community. Allameh Tabatabei refers to velayat as a political philosophy for Shia and velayat faqih for Shia community. There are also other attempts to formulate a new attitude of Islam such as the publication of three volumes of Maktab Tashayyo. Also some believe that it is indispensable to revive the religious gathered in Hoseyniyeh-e-Ershad.To expand this revolution many cleric's from several nation are working hard. From Pakistan Allama Syed Jawad Naqvi is the prominent shia cleric defending and promoting Islamic Revolution and the ideology of Wilayat Faqeeh.[^cite_note-379]

## Depictions in Western media

Main article: [List of Iranian Revolution films](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Iranian_Revolution_films)

- *[Whirlwind](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Whirlwind_\(novel\))*, a novel by [James Clavell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Clavell) (1986), portrays the events in February and March, 1979, involving the characters from the fictional company [Noble House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asian_Saga). Most of the locations in the novel are real, and historical personalities are frequently mentioned in the background story.

- *[Argo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Argo_\(2012_film\))*, starring [Ben Affleck](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ben_Affleck), dramatizes the execution of the [Canadian Caper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadian_Caper) during the [Iranian hostage crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_hostage_crisis).

- *[House of Sand and Fog](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Sand_and_Fog_\(film\))*, starring [Jennifer Connelly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jennifer_Connelly), is a fictional portrayal of an exiled Iranian military officer and his family.

- *[Persepolis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persepolis_\(comic\))* is an autobiographical series of comics by [Marjane Satrapi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marjane_Satrapi) first published in 2000 that depicts the author's childhood in Iran during and after the Islamic Revolution. The 2007 animated film *[Persepolis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persepolis_\(film\))* is based upon on it.

- *[Septembers of Shiraz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Septembers_of_Shiraz)* is a movie about an Iranian Jewish family. After creating a prosperous life in Iran, they may be forced to abandon everything as a revolution looms on the horizon. It is based on the 2007 novel *[The Septembers of Shiraz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Septembers_of_Shiraz)* by [Dalia Sofer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dalia_Sofer).

- *[1979 Revolution: Black Friday](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1979_Revolution:_Black_Friday)*, a 2016 video game set in the backdrop of the Iranian Revolution.

- *[Iran and the West](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran_and_the_West)*, a three-part British documentary series shown in February 2009 on the [BBC](19344654.md).

[^cite_note-38]: &#x20;Özbudun, Ergun (2011). "Authoritarian Regimes". In Badie, Bertrand; Berg-Schlosser, Dirk; Morlino, Leonardo (eds.). *International Encyclopedia of Political Science*. SAGE. p. 109. [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [978-1-4522-6649-7](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/978-1-4522-6649-7). Another interesting borderline case between authoritarianism and totalitarianism is Iran, where an almost totalitarian interpretation of a religious ideology is combined with elements of limited pluralism. Under the Islamist regime, Islam has been transformed into a political ideology with a totalitarian bent, and the limited pluralism is allowed only among political groups loyal to the Islamic revolution.

Accessed March 8, 2026. "An exultant Yasir Arafat, leader of the Palestine Liberation Organization, proclaimed here today that the Iranian revolution had 'turned upside down' the balance of forces in the Middle East. 'Today Iran, tomorrow Palestine,' he said. Mr. Arafat received a pledge from Ayatollah Khomeini that the Iranians would 'turn to the issue of victory over Israel' after Iran had consolidated its strength, the Teheran radio reported.... Bantering and grinning, the guerrilla leader declined to furnish details about support the P.L.O. had given to various Iranian guerrilla organizations, saying: 'It is enough that we are here, and no matter how much we have helped we cannot offer as much back as the Iranian people have offered us.'"

Accessed March 8, 2026. "Under the Pahlavi dynasty, which ruled from 1925 until it was overthrown in the 1979 revolution, ties between Iran and Israel were anything but hostile.... Israel established a de facto embassy in Tehran, and eventually they exchanged ambassadors in the 1970s. Trade ties grew, and soon Iran became a major oil provider for Israel, with the two establishing a pipeline aimed at sending Iranian oil to Israel and then Europe. Tehran and Tel Aviv also had extensive military and security cooperation, but it was largely kept under wraps to avoid provoking the Arab nations in the region."
